Under the McGucken Principle, the principal U(1)-bundle whose connection is A_μ admits a globally-defined reference section — the direction specified by dx₄/dt = +ic, uniform across all spacetime. Any principal U(1)-bundle admitting a global section is trivial: P ≅ ℳ × U(1). The first Chern class c_1(P) therefore vanishes, and no magnetic monopoles can exist in the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework. The absence of monopoles is a rigorous bundle-triviality theorem, not a phenomenological observation.

Proof in [MG-QED, §VIII.3]. The argument is standard differential geometry applied to the specific LTD context. A magnetic monopole at a point p ∈ ℳ corresponds geometrically to a non-trivial U(1)-bundle over ℳ − {p}, with the monopole-surrounding 2-sphere S² carrying first Chern class ∫_{S²} F/(2π) = g ≠ 0 (the magnetic charge). A principal G-bundle P → ℳ is trivial if and only if it admits a continuous global section σ: ℳ → P; for G = U(1), such a section assigns a U(1)-phase angle to each spacetime point. Under the McGucken Principle, the direction +ic of dx₄/dt is uniform across all of spacetime — it is the postulate’s specification — and therefore supplies a globally-defined reference phase at every point, constituting a continuous global section of the x₄-orientation bundle. By the equivalence of global section and bundle triviality, the bundle is P = ℳ × U(1), c_1(P) = 0, and no magnetic charge can exist. This distinguishes the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework from Grand Unified Theories, in which monopoles arise via the ‘t Hooft-Polyakov mechanism at the GUT scale (~10^15 GeV): GUT monopoles are suppressed but not excluded. The McGucken prediction is absolute: no monopoles at any energy scale. A single monopole observation would refute the McGucken Principle directly by forcing the +ic direction to be non-uniform across some spacetime region.

#### Remark III.5.1 (SU(2)_L and SU(3)_c as geometric extensions of the x₄-orientation construction)

The Proposition III.5 derivation of the U(1) gauge structure from local x₄-phase invariance suggests an extension to the non-Abelian gauge groups SU(2)_L and SU(3)_c of the electroweak and strong sectors. The program of [MG-Noether, §§VII.1-VII.2] and [MG-Broken] proposed such extensions via two distinct geometric sectors of the four-dimensional manifold on which x₄ expands, and those proposals are completed to derivations in [MG-QFT, §9]. The route runs through an internal algebra 𝒜_F = ℂ ⊕ ℍ ⊕ M₃(ℂ), each summand a substrate-scale feature of the advance — the ℂ summand the x₄-phase, the ℍ summand the Cl(1,3)⁺ Weyl-doublet structure of the McGucken-Sphere SO(3) lift, the M₃(ℂ) summand the three spatial directions — with the gauge group of each summand its projective inner-automorphism group. What remains empirical is stated at §VIII.25.6: the three-generation structure, the mixing matrices with the CP-violating phase, and the numerical values of the couplings, the vacuum expectation value and the individual Yukawas.

The candidate interpretation proceeds as follows. At the Euclidean level before imposing x₄ = ict, the four-dimensional rotation group has double cover Spin(4) ≅ SU(2)_L × SU(2)_R — a factorization peculiar to four dimensions [MG-Dirac, §III]. Under the McGucken Principle, the +ic direction is globally fixed, and the subgroup of Spin(4) that stabilizes this direction is Spin(3) ≅ SU(2); this is the candidate geometric origin of weak SU(2)_L. The color group SU(3)_c is less directly geometric: the three spatial dimensions x₁, x₂, x₃ equally transverse to x₄ form a symmetric triplet whose real-rotation group is SO(3), not SU(3) — so SU(3)_c cannot be the spatial-rotation group itself. The candidate geometric reading of [MG-Noether, §VII.2] interprets SU(3)_c as the unitary group acting on a complex triplet structure constructed from the three spatial axes, with the specific group content requiring additional input beyond what the McGucken Principle supplies on its own.

The overall program target is to close each candidate interpretation into a first-principles derivation of the specific group, matching the derivation [MG-SM, Theorems 10-11] provides for the general Yang-Mills Lagrangian given a compact Lie group G. At present, this program is complete for: (i) the existence of U(1) gauge invariance (from x₄-phase indeterminacy, Proposition III.5 and [MG-SM, Theorem 5]); (ii) the general Yang-Mills Lagrangian structure for any compact Lie group G ([MG-SM, Theorems 10-11]); (iii) the chiral coupling pattern (left-handed fermions to SU(2)_L, from matter orientation condition (M) and [MG-Dirac, §A.2 via MG-Broken]). The program is incomplete for: (iv) the derivation of SU(2)_L as the specific weak gauge group from first principles, (v) the derivation of SU(3)_c as the specific color group from first principles, and (vi) the reason nature selected gauge group U(1) × SU(2) × SU(3) rather than any other compact Lie group consistent with the observed matter content. Items (iv), (v), (vi) are established in [MG-QFT, §9], which completes the candidate geometric readings outlined above: SU(2)_L as the McGucken-Sphere SO(3) lift on Cl(1,3)⁺ Weyl doublets, SU(3)_c = PInn(M₃(ℂ)) with the three colours the three spatial directions, and the product group as the automorphism content of the internal algebra 𝒜_F = ℂ ⊕ ℍ ⊕ M₃(ℂ) forced by substrate-feature exhaustion, with a No-GUT result excluding any larger embedding. Proposition VI.2 of the present paper assumes the gauge group G as input (following [MG-SM, Theorem 11]) and proves uniqueness of the kinetic Lagrangian given G; the group-selection question is separate and open.

Within the strong-sector piece, one concrete conjectural link: the symmetric action of x₄’s advance on the three spatial dimensions (no preferred direction among x₁, x₂, x₃) is a geometric constraint consistent with — and, per [MG-Broken]’s proposed derivation, providing the reason for — strong CP conservation θ_QCD < 10⁻¹⁰. This connection between x₄’s symmetric spatial action and the absence of QCD θ-term physics is the kind of derivational move the program aspires to complete for each gauge-group factor.

#### Remark III.5.2 (The i as algebraic signature of perpendicularity to the spatial dimensions)

A observation establishing the geometric status of the imaginary unit throughout ℒ_McG [MG-Noether, §VII.5]: the factor i that appears in dx₄/dt = ic, in the matter orientation condition Ψ(x, x₄) = Ψ₀(x)·exp(+I·kx₄), in the covariant derivative D_μ = ∂_μ − ieA_μ, in the canonical commutation relation [q, p] = iℏ, in the Schrödinger equation iℏ∂ψ/∂t = Ĥψ, in the Feynman path-integral weight e^(iS/ℏ), in the +iε prescription for propagators, in the Dirac equation (iγ^μ∂_μ − m)ψ = 0, in the Heisenberg equation dA/dt = (i/ℏ)[H, A], in the Wick rotation t → −iτ, in the complex wave function ψ(x, t), in the Fourier transform kernel e^(−ipx/ℏ), in the Fresnel integral rotation e^(iπ/4), in the unitary evolution operator U = e^(−iHt/ℏ), and in the Euclidean-Minkowski action relation iS_M = −S_E is, in every instance, the algebraic signature of perpendicularity to the three spatial dimensions — not an imaginariness in any ontological sense, but the perpendicularity operator in an algebraic framework. Multiplication by i rotates a vector by 90° in the complex plane; in the equation x₄ = ict, the i asserts that the fourth coordinate is perpendicular to the coordinate time parameter t measured in the spatial dimensions. In every equation of quantum theory, the i appearing “by hand” is the fourth dimension’s calling card, left in the formalism by Minkowski (x₄ = ict, 1908), Schrödinger (iℏ∂/∂t, 1926), Dirac ([q, p] = iℏ and iγ^μ∂_μ, 1928-1930), Heisenberg ((i/ℏ)[H, A], 1927), Feynman (e^(iS/ℏ), 1948), and Wick (t → −iτ, 1954) before the physical fourth dimension underlying all of them had been recognized. What the standard quantum formalism treated as a technical feature requiring no physical explanation — the ubiquitous appearance of i throughout the equations — is the geometric expression of the fourth dimension asserting itself in every equation of twentieth-century physics. The complexification of quantum theory is the direct algebraic signature of x₄ in the formalism. The parallel between dx₄/dt = ic and [q, p] = iℏ is an identity, not an analogy: both equations express the same geometric fact, with i marking perpendicularity, c as the rate of x₄’s advance, and ℏ as the quantum of action per x₄-expansion step [MG-Commut, §V.8]. The McGucken Lagrangian ℒ_McG inherits this identification: every i appearing in its matter, gauge, and gravitational sectors is a projection onto x₄, derived rather than postulated.

### III.7 The ADM Foliation and Curved Spacetime

Proposition III.6 (ADM foliation of spacetime by constant-x₄ hypersurfaces).

Under the McGucken Principle, spacetime admits a natural foliation by the level surfaces of x₄. Each leaf is a spatial three-manifold Σ_{x₄} on which a spatial metric h_ij is defined, and the metric on the full spacetime is decomposed in ADM form:

ds² = −N² dt² + h_ij(dx^i + N^i dt)(dx^j + N^j dt),

with N the lapse and N^i the shift, both functions of the foliation. In the curved-spacetime generalization of the McGucken Principle, x₄’s advance along any worldline is modulated by the local spatial curvature, giving the Einstein field equations as the dynamical consistency condition.

Proof in [MG-GR, §II]. The ADM foliation is the natural geometric structure on which x₄’s advance is defined in curved spacetime, and the Einstein field equations follow as the condition that the advance be consistent (unique and well-defined) across the foliation. The curved-spacetime version of the McGucken Principle is therefore equivalent to general relativity; the specific connection is that x₄ = ict in flat spacetime is replaced by x₄ = ∫dτ along the worldline in curved spacetime, with proper time τ measured by the metric. The McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework provides the physically preferred choice of time slicing within the ADM framework: the x₄-foliation, in which N^i = 0 (zero shift — no dragging of spatial coordinates) and N = √(−g₀₀) encodes the ratio of x₄-advance to coordinate time. In this preferred foliation, ds² = −N²c²dt² + h_ij dx^i dx^j, with the lapse function N encoding the gravitational time dilation and the spatial metric h_ij encoding the gravitational length distortion — one dynamical variable (h_ij) with a geometrically natural clock (N set by x₄’s advance).

#### Remark III.6.1 (The metric tensor as refractive index for x₄’s advance)

A central physical identification established in [MG-GR, §X] sharpens the interpretation of g_μν used throughout §VI and §VIII of the present paper. The metric tensor g_μν is the distributed refractive index of three-dimensional space for x₄’s invariant expansion: where space is curved by the presence of mass, x₄’s expansion wavefront must traverse a longer optical path. The lapse function N = √(−g₀₀) is the local ratio of proper time to coordinate time — the local rate at which x₄ advances relative to its flat-space rate of ic. Near a mass, N < 1: x₄’s advance is retarded by the stretched spatial geometry, exactly as light is retarded by an optically dense medium. In Schwarzschild geometry, n(r) = 1/N = (1 − r_s/r)^(−1/2) matches Gordon’s optical metric exactly [Gordon 1923]: the gravitational field of mass M acts as an optical medium with this refractive index for the propagation of x₄’s expansion, and photons (which surf x₄’s expansion) travel along the null geodesics of this effective optical medium. The bending of light near a massive object is identical to the bending of light in an optical medium with a radially varying refractive index — producing the observed deflection angle δθ = 4GM/(bc²). This is the physical identification that underlies the uniqueness of the Einstein-Hilbert action in Proposition VI.3 below.

#### Remark III.6.2 (The Schwarzschild metric as a theorem of the McGucken Principle)

The Schwarzschild metric is derived in six explicit steps from dx₄/dt = ic alone [MG-GR, §X.2], using no additional assumptions beyond (i) x₄’s invariant expansion rate ic, (ii) spherical symmetry of the expansion from each point, (iii) asymptotic flatness, and (iv) Gauss’s law for the gravitational source. These four constraints uniquely fix: the temporal metric component N² = (1 − r_s/r) (from the refractive-index consistency condition that x₄’s invariant expansion traverses the stretched spatial geometry consistently); the radial spatial metric component h_rr = 1/(1 − r_s/r) (from Gauss’s law applied to the stretched spatial geometry, equivalent to the statement that spatial stretching is proportional to the Newtonian potential Φ = −GM/r); and the angular components h_θθ = r², h_φφ = r²sin²θ (preserved by spherical symmetry). The product N² · h_rr = 1 expresses the conservation of x₄’s expansion rate: what is lost in temporal advance is gained in spatial stretching. Birkhoff’s theorem — that the Schwarzschild metric is the unique spherically symmetric vacuum solution of Einstein’s equations — is therefore the mathematical expression of the four McGucken constraints above. This derivation route is independent of the Lovelock uniqueness argument of Proposition VI.3 below; the two routes converge on the same gravitational sector from different directions.

#### Remark III.6.3 (The stress-energy tensor as a map of x₄-resistance)

The stress-energy tensor T_μν of the present paper’s matter and gauge sectors (which couple to g_μν through minimal coupling in curved spacetime) has a direct physical interpretation under the McGucken Principle [MG-GR, §X.4]: T_μν is a map of where and how strongly x₄’s invariant expansion is being resisted, diverted, or impeded by the presence of matter and energy. The (0,0) component T₀₀ = ρc² is the energy density — the concentration of x₄-impedance per unit spatial volume; the spatial diagonal Tᵢᵢ = P is the pressure — the isotropic flux of x₄-stretching that a fluid element exerts on the spatial metric; the off-diagonal T₀ᵢ encodes the directionality of x₄-advance. Einstein’s equations G_μν = (8πG/c⁴)T_μν then read: the left side is the deformation of x₄’s spherically symmetric wavefront (spatial curvature); the right side is the resistance to x₄’s advance (matter-energy density and flux); the coupling 8πG/c⁴ converts resistance into wavefront deformation. The Einstein field equations are the equations of motion for x₄’s wavefront propagating through a three-dimensional universe of matter that resists its advance. This reading supplies the physical interpretation that the Lovelock uniqueness theorem of Proposition VI.3 establishes only at the level of algebraic form.

### III.8 The Dual-Channel Content of the Principle: Channel A and Channel B

Everything below descends from the physical expansion of x₄. The McGucken Principle states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the rate c from every point of spacetime, dx₄/dt = ic. This section separates two pieces of information carried by that single statement, because the separation organizes every uniqueness proof of §§IV–VI, the two-route derivation of §VIII.6.7, and the classification of prior frameworks in §VIII.22. The separation and its consequences for the foundations of quantum mechanics are developed at length in the companion paper [MG-TwoRoutes, §V]; what follows states the part of it the present paper uses.

#### III.8.1 The two contents, separated

The full physical statement of the Principle is that x₄ advances at the invariant rate ic, from every spacetime point, spherically symmetrically about each point. Two logically distinct pieces of information are contained in that sentence.

**Channel A — the algebraic-symmetry content.** The advance has a uniform rate ic that is invariant under the isometries of spacetime: the same at every spacetime point (translation invariance), independent of direction among the three spatial dimensions (rotation invariance), and form-invariant under Lorentz boost (boost invariance). The content of this channel is uniformity together with invariance. It is what supplies the Minkowski metric of Proposition III.1 by substitution, the four-speed budget of Proposition III.2 by Lorentz covariance, the ten Poincaré conservation laws of Remark III.4.1 through Noether's theorem, and the local phase invariance of Proposition III.5 through the absence of a preferred orthogonal reference. It is the content to which Stone's theorem applies, since Stone's theorem is a statement about strongly continuous unitary representations of a symmetry group.

**Channel B — the geometric-propagation content.** The advance is spherically symmetric about every spacetime point: it radiates equally into all spatial directions from each point of emission at rate c. The content of this channel is emission from every point with radial rate c. It is what supplies the McGucken Sphere, the forward light cone as the locus of spheres of radius c(t − t₀), Huygens' secondary-wavelet structure, the retarded Green's function supported on that locus, and the path set of the Feynman construction under iteration. It is the content to which wavefront and caustic arguments apply, since those are statements about propagation rather than about symmetry.

Both channels are present in the one statement, and neither is derived from the other. "Advancing at ic from every point" is the algebraic-symmetry content — a uniform rate, invariant under the isometries. "Spherically symmetrically about each point" is the geometric-propagation content — an isotropic wavefront inheriting Huygens' secondary-wavelet property. The two are not competing readings of the Principle; they are simultaneously valid readings, each unpacking a different aspect of the same geometric fact.

Remark III.8.1 (why a real advancing axis carries both). The two contents are logically independent — one can write down, as a formal exercise, a uniform-rate advance that is not spherically symmetric, or a spherically symmetric advance whose rate is not uniform — yet both are required components of dx₄/dt = ic read as a physical statement about a real geometric axis. A real axis in a homogeneous and isotropic three-space, when it advances, advances from every point (uniformity) in every direction (isotropy). The dual content is therefore forced by the minimal physical reading of the mathematical statement rather than added to it, and this is the reason the Principle possesses a property that no framework treating geometry as a static stage can possess (§VIII.20).

#### III.8.2 Which channel drives which result in the present paper

The separation is not decorative; the uniqueness subtheorems of §§IV–VI divide along it, and stating the division makes visible what each proof actually consumes.

Proposition IV.1, the uniqueness of the free-particle functional, consumes Channel A. Its conditions are Poincaré invariance, reparametrization invariance, locality, first-order derivatives and dimensional consistency, and the Poincaré invariance is the isometry content of the uniform rate.

Proposition V.1, the uniqueness of the Dirac matter sector, consumes Channel A through the Lorentz-scalar and parity requirements and the Clifford algebra forced by the Minkowski signature, together with Channel B through the matter orientation condition (M), whose Compton-frequency coupling is a coupling to the oscillatory advance rather than to its symmetry.

Proposition VI.2, the uniqueness of the Yang-Mills sector, consumes Channel A. Local gauge invariance follows from x₄-phase indeterminacy — the Principle fixes magnitude and direction and fixes no orthogonal reference — which is a statement about what the symmetry does not distinguish.

Proposition VI.3, the uniqueness of the Einstein-Hilbert sector, consumes Channel B in its Schuller-closure form. The input to the closure equations is the universal principal polynomial P(k) = η^μν k_μ k_ν, which is the causal cone — the propagation structure — shared by every matter sector. The Jacobson route of Remark VI.3.1 consumes Channel B likewise, through the horizon mode count on null hypersurfaces.

Proposition IV.3, the field equations of the free sector, consumes both: the constraint is the Channel A budget, and the congruence whose caustics bound the construction (§IV.4.4) is a Channel B object.

The four-fold uniqueness theorem therefore rests on both channels, and this is what distinguishes it from a uniqueness result obtainable from a symmetry principle alone. A framework carrying only Channel A can force the gauge and matter kinetic terms given a group and a signature, and cannot reach the propagation structure that Schuller closure requires as input. A framework carrying only Channel B can produce wavefronts and a path measure, and cannot reach the operator algebra. The Principle carries both, which is why Theorem VI.1 ranges over four sectors rather than one.

#### III.8.3 The channel taxonomy applied to prior frameworks

Classifying a candidate foundation by its channel content is a compact way of saying what it can and cannot reach, and the classification is used in §VIII.22 and in the survey of [MG-TwoRoutes, §VI]. Three examples fix the usage.

Classical symplectic geometry — a phase space (M, ω) with Hamiltonian vector fields defined by ω(X_H, ·) = dH — carries Channel A content in the algebraic structure of the symplectic form and carries no Channel B content, because a symplectic manifold has no advance of any kind and therefore nothing that propagates. Geometric quantization builds a Hilbert space and operators from (M, ω) and reaches the operator formulation; the path integral has to be constructed separately rather than emerging.

Stochastic mechanics carries partial Channel B content — a propagation structure on configuration space — and no Channel A content, and correspondingly reaches the Schrödinger equation without separately deriving the commutation relation or the representation structure.

Geometric algebra carries static Channel B-like content, in that it supplies a geometric identity for the imaginary unit, and no dynamical principle generating either channel; it reinterprets rather than derives.

The pattern across the fifteen frameworks surveyed in [MG-TwoRoutes, §VI] is that each carries partial content of one channel, or neither, and that no prior candidate foundation carries both channels simultaneously in a single geometric-dynamical statement. Section VIII.22 applies the same classification to the Assumptions of Physics programme, whose extended-phase-space construction is Channel A throughout.

## IV. The Free-Particle Sector and Its Uniqueness

This section derives the free-particle Lagrangian for a worldline under the McGucken Principle and proves its uniqueness.

### IV.1 The Free-Particle Action

Consider a classical massive particle of rest mass m tracing a worldline γ in Minkowski spacetime. By the McGucken Principle (§III.1), x₄ advances along the worldline at rate ic in the rest frame, and at the Lorentz-boosted rate in moving frames, subject to the four-speed budget u^μu_μ = −c² (§III.3). The accumulated magnitude of x₄’s advance along the worldline is

|Δx₄|_γ = ∫_γ |dx₄|,

and this is the geometrically natural action functional of the worldline under the McGucken Principle: the functional measures how much x₄-advance the worldline accumulates. With the dimensionally correct factor −mc out front (mass × velocity, yielding action units), the free-particle action is

S_free = −mc ∫_γ |dx₄| = −mc ∫_γ √(−ds²) = −mc² ∫_γ dτ,

where ds² = η_μν dx^μ dx^ν is the Minkowski line element (Proposition III.1) and τ is the proper time along the worldline. The three expressions for S_free are equivalent: the first is in the language of x₄-advance, the second is in the language of the Minkowski metric, and the third is in the language of proper time — all three are the same functional of the worldline, written in different notational forms. The second and third forms are the standard relativistic free-particle action that appears in every textbook on special relativity; the first form, in the language of |dx₄|, is the form that exposes the geometric origin of the action.

### IV.2 The Euler-Lagrange Equation of the Free Worldline

Varying S_free with respect to the worldline x^μ(λ) — with λ any parametrization — gives the Euler-Lagrange equation

d/dτ (mc u^μ) = 0, u^μ u_μ = −c²,

which is the relativistic free-particle equation of motion: the four-momentum p^μ = mc u^μ is conserved along the worldline, and the four-velocity norm is fixed at −c² (Proposition III.2). In the rest frame of the particle (u^1 = u^2 = u^3 = 0), the constraint gives u^4 = ic, recovering the McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic as the rest-frame equation of motion. The free-particle Lagrangian is therefore consistent with the McGucken Principle, and the Principle itself is the rest-frame statement of the Lagrangian’s equation of motion.

### IV.3 The Uniqueness Theorem

Proposition IV.1 (Uniqueness of the free-particle action).

Let γ be a timelike worldline in Minkowski spacetime and let S[γ] be a real scalar functional of γ satisfying: (a) Poincaré invariance — S[γ] is invariant under the full Poincaré group (Lorentz transformations plus translations) of spacetime, so that no spacetime point is preferred over any other; (b) reparametrization invariance — S[γ] depends on γ only through its image as a curve in ℳ, not on the choice of parameter λ labeling points on γ; (c) locality — S[γ] is the integral along γ of a local functional of position and first derivatives, S[γ] = ∫_γ F(x^μ, ẋ^μ) dλ; (d) first-order derivatives only — F depends on ẋ^μ but not on ẍ^μ or higher derivatives; (e) dimensional consistency — S has units of action (energy × time). Under these five conditions, the unique (up to overall multiplicative constant and additive total-derivative terms) functional S[γ] is

S[γ] = −mc ∫_γ √(−η_μν ẋ^μ ẋ^ν) dλ = −mc ∫_γ |dx₄| = −mc² ∫_γ dτ,

with m a constant of dimension mass. No other functional of the worldline satisfies all five conditions.

Proof.

The argument is a standard result in the calculus of variations, adapted here to the Lorentzian worldline setting. By condition (c), S[γ] = ∫ F(x, ẋ) dλ for some local F. By condition (b) (reparametrization invariance), F must be homogeneous of degree one in ẋ^μ: F(x, αẋ) = αF(x, ẋ) for α > 0. To see this, consider a reparametrization λ → λ'(λ) with dλ’ = α·dλ for constant α > 0 (local rescaling; the argument extends to general monotonic reparametrizations). Then the velocity in the new parameter is ẋ_new:= dx/dλ’ = (dx/dλ)·(dλ/dλ’) = ẋ/α, and the measure is dλ = dλ’/α. The integrand F(x, ẋ)dλ expressed in the new parameter becomes F(x, α·ẋ_new)·(dλ’/α). Reparametrization invariance requires this equal F(x, ẋ_new)·dλ’, so F(x, α·ẋ_new) = α·F(x, ẋ_new) for all α > 0 — F is homogeneous of degree one in its velocity argument. By condition (a) (Lorentz invariance), F must be a Lorentz scalar built from ẋ^μ and (possibly) η_μν. The most general such F that is homogeneous of degree one in ẋ is

F(x, ẋ) = A(x) √(−η_μν ẋ^μ ẋ^ν) + B_μ(x) ẋ^μ,

where A(x) is a Lorentz scalar and B_μ(x) is a Lorentz covector. The first term — proportional to √(−ẋ^μẋ_μ) — is homogeneous of degree one and Lorentz-invariant; the second term — proportional to ẋ^μ itself — is also homogeneous of degree one and Lorentz-covariant when contracted with a covector. Exhaustiveness: any other degree-one scalar built from ẋ with η-contractions must be an odd power of ẋ·ẋ, but only the half-power case is degree one, giving the first term; degree-one-in-ẋ scalars with external covector structure must contract ẋ against a rank-one object, giving the second term. The two-term form above is the most general allowed by (a)-(d).

By condition (d) (no higher-order derivatives) and condition (c) (locality), neither A(x) nor B_μ(x) can depend on ẋ^μ. For a free particle — by hypothesis, no external fields couple to the particle — the B_μ(x)ẋ^μ term must either vanish or reduce to a boundary term. The reason is that a nonzero field-strength F_μν = ∂_μ B_ν − ∂_ν B_μ would produce, under variation of the action, an equation of motion d/dλ(∂F/∂ẋ^μ) − ∂F/∂x^μ = 0 containing the term F_μν ẋ^ν. This is the Lorentz force on a particle of unit charge in an external electromagnetic potential A_μ = B_μ (Landau-Lifshitz §16). The free-particle assumption therefore forces F_μν = 0, i.e. the covector B_μ is closed. By the Poincaré lemma on the Minkowski manifold, a closed covector on a contractible domain is exact: B_μ = ∂_μφ for some scalar function φ. The resulting contribution to S is ∫ ∂_μφ · ẋ^μ dλ = ∫ dφ/dλ · dλ = φ(end) − φ(start), a pure boundary term. Boundary terms do not affect the Euler-Lagrange equations and can be dropped from S without loss of generality. Therefore B_μ is effectively zero for the free-particle action, and

F(x, ẋ) = A √(−η_μν ẋ^μ ẋ^ν)

with A a constant. (The x-independence of A follows from the translation-invariance piece of condition (a): Poincaré invariance forbids A from depending on x^μ, since any x-dependent A(x) would distinguish some spacetime point as the origin of the function. The x-independence of B_μ similarly follows, before the free-particle argument reduces it to a pure gradient.) By condition (e) (dimensional consistency), A must have units of action divided by time times velocity — that is, units of mass × velocity. Writing A = −mc (with the conventional sign chosen to make the Euler-Lagrange equations agree with the standard relativistic free-particle equations, and with m a mass parameter), we obtain

S[γ] = −mc ∫_γ √(−η_μν ẋ^μ ẋ^ν) dλ,

which is the claimed unique functional. The equivalent forms S = −mc ∫|dx₄| and S = −mc² ∫dτ follow from Proposition III.1 (ds² from dx₄/dt = ic) and the definition of proper time dτ² = −ds²/c².

∎

#### Remark IV.1 (Connection to Lovelock uniqueness)

Proposition IV.1 is the worldline analogue of Lovelock’s uniqueness theorem for the gravitational action. Lovelock (1971) proved that in four spacetime dimensions, the Einstein-Hilbert action is the unique diffeomorphism-invariant scalar action on the metric that produces second-order field equations. Proposition IV.1 proves that on a timelike worldline, the free-particle action −mc ∫|dx₄| is the unique Lorentz-invariant, reparametrization-invariant scalar action that produces first-order field equations. The two uniqueness theorems have the same structure — given a symmetry group and an order-of-derivatives requirement, the action is forced. Together they establish that the kinetic sectors of the McGucken Lagrangian are not chosen but forced by the invariance requirements.

#### Remark IV.2 (The meaning of the minus sign and the factor m)

The minus sign in −mc ∫|dx₄| is conventional: with this sign the Euler-Lagrange equation d/dτ (mc u^μ) = 0 has the standard form, and the energy of a particle at rest is +mc² (not −mc²). The factor m is the rest mass of the particle, with dimension mass — the only parameter in the free-particle sector of ℒ_McG. No other parameter is free: c is determined by the McGucken Principle (|dx₄/dt| = c), and ℏ is determined by the oscillatory form of the principle (ℏ = ℓ_P² c³/G = action per Planck-frequency cycle of x₄). The free-particle sector therefore contains exactly one free parameter per species of matter — the rest mass — which matches the empirics of special relativity (one rest mass per particle species).

#### Remark IV.3 (Conserved currents via Noether’s theorem)

By Noether’s theorem (§II.5), the symmetries of S_free generate conservation laws. Translation invariance in x^μ gives conservation of four-momentum p^μ = mc u^μ. Rotational invariance in the spatial triple (x¹, x², x³) gives conservation of angular momentum L^ij = x^i p^j − x^j p^i. Boost invariance gives conservation of the boost charges K^i = tp^i − x^i E/c². These conservation laws — the Poincaré charges of special relativity — are derived in full in [MG-Noether, §§IV–V] as consequences of the symmetries of the McGucken free-particle action, with the McGucken Principle supplying the physics of each symmetry (temporal translation invariance as uniformity of dx₄/dt = ic; spatial translation invariance as homogeneity of the McGucken Sphere; rotational invariance as the spherical symmetry of x₄’s advance).

### IV.4 The x₄-Advance Field and the Field Form of the Free Sector

Everything below descends from the physical expansion of x₄. The McGucken Principle states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the rate c from every point of spacetime, dx₄/dt = ic. Sections IV.1–IV.3 read that expansion along one worldline and obtained the action S_free = −mc ∫_γ |dx₄| together with its uniqueness (Proposition IV.1). The present section reads the same expansion across the whole manifold at once. Because x₄ advances from every event and not only along a chosen curve, the advance is a field on ℳ, and the free sector of ℒ_McG is the Lagrangian density of that field. This section names the field, writes its density, derives its three field equations, and proves that the worldline action of Proposition IV.1 is the restriction of that density to a single integral curve. No postulate is added: what follows is the McGucken Principle written as a statement about a field rather than about a curve.

#### IV.4.1 What the free sector requires of a density

The McGucken Principle asserts dx₄/dt = ic at every event p ∈ ℳ. The object it constrains is therefore defined at every event, and three requirements follow for any density that is to carry it.

The first is agreement of domain. ℒ_matter, ℒ_gauge and ℒ_grav are densities integrated over d⁴x. The functional S_free = −mc ∫_γ |dx₄| of Proposition IV.1 is a one-dimensional integral along a curve, and the parameter m appearing in it belongs to a single particle rather than to a field configuration on ℳ. A curve integral enters a sum with spacetime integrals after it has been written as one.

The second concerns the interaction between the constraint and the term it constrains. Writing the free sector as −mc √(−∂_μx₄ ∂^μx₄) while imposing ∂_μx₄ ∂^μx₄ = −c² on every configuration makes the square root equal to a constant, so that the term reduces to −mc² and leaves every field equation unchanged apart from an additive shift of the cosmological term. The worldline dynamics of §IV.2 are recovered when the constraint is imposed by variation rather than substituted before variation.

The third concerns the carrier of the constraint. A condition on the fields enters a variational formulation through a multiplier, or else through elimination of a variable. Stated as a side condition alone, ∂_μx₄ ∂^μx₄ = −c² has no representative in the action and generates no field equation of its own, so the four-speed budget of Proposition III.2 — which is the covariant expression of the McGucken Principle — would enter ℒ_McG as an annotation rather than as dynamics.

The three requirements are met together by the construction of §IV.4.2. The construction is a consequence of the Principle: x₄ advances from every event, so its accumulated advance is a function on ℳ, and the rate of that advance is fixed at c, so the gradient of that function has fixed norm.

A note on units and signature, stated once and used throughout this section. Coordinates are x^μ = (x₁, x₂, x₃, x⁰) with x⁰ = ct, all four carrying the dimension of length, and η_μν = diag(+, +, +, −) as in Proposition III.1, so that ds² = dx₁² + dx₂² + dx₃² − c²dt². The perpendicularity marker i is carried by the coordinate x₄ = ict of §III.1, and X below is the same advance written on the real axis, x₄ = iX. In these coordinates a gradient of a length is dimensionless, so the four-speed budget reads ∂_μX ∂^μX = −1, which is the condition ∂_μx₄ ∂^μx₄ = −c² of Theorem VI.1 written with x⁰ = ct in place of t in the μ = 0 slot. The two are the same condition; the second form carries the factor c² because it differentiates with respect to coordinate time.

The measure is fixed separately from the index convention, and the two choices are independent. Densities in ℒ_McG are integrated as ∫ ℒ dt d³x, so every density carries the dimension of energy per unit volume. This is the convention under which the gravitational prefactor of Proposition VI.3 reads c⁴/16πG: with R of dimension m⁻² and the measure of dimension s·m³, the product (c⁴/16πG)·R·(dt d³x) has the dimension of action. Writing the measure instead as dx₁dx₂dx₃dx⁰, with all four differentials lengths, moves one factor of c out of every density and returns the equivalent prefactor c³/16πG; both forms appear in the literature and they describe the same action. The present paper uses the first throughout, and every density below — including the free-sector density of Definition IV.4.2 — is an energy density in that convention.

#### IV.4.2 The x₄-advance field, its constraint, and its density

Everything below descends from the physical expansion of x₄. Because the advance proceeds at rate c from every event, the accumulated advance is a single-valued function of position wherever the congruence of advance is caustic-free, and the rate c fixes the norm of its gradient.

Definition IV.4.1 (the x₄-advance field). Let X: ℳ → ℝ assign to each event p the accumulated magnitude of x₄'s advance from a fixed initial hypersurface Σ₀ to p, measured along the integral curve of the advance that reaches p:

X(p) = ∫ |dx₄|.

X carries the dimension of length. Its level sets are the constant-x₄ hypersurfaces Σ_{x₄} of the foliation of Proposition III.6, and its gradient is the covector field normal to that foliation.

Proposition IV.2 (the four-speed budget as a constraint on X).

Under the McGucken Principle, the x₄-advance field satisfies

∂_μX ∂^μX = −1. (C)

Proof. Along any integral curve of the advance, dX = |dx₄| = c dτ by §IV.1, so X is c times the proper time of that curve, and X is therefore the arclength function of the congruence with respect to the interval −ds²/c². The gradient of an arclength function has unit norm with respect to the metric that measures it, which for a timelike congruence in signature (+, +, +, −) reads ∂_μX ∂^μX = −1. Equivalently: the four-velocity of the congruence is u^μ = −c ∂^μX, and Proposition III.2 gives u^μu_μ = −c², so c² ∂_μX ∂^μX = −c². ∎

Remark IV.4 (the constraint is the relativistic Hamilton–Jacobi equation). Writing S = −mcX turns (C) into

g^μν ∂_μS ∂_νS = −m²c²,

which is the mass-shell relation p^μp_μ = −m²c² of Proposition III.4(ii) with p_μ = ∂_μS. The McGucken reading of this identity: what classical mechanics names Hamilton's principal function is −mc times the accumulated advance of the fourth dimension, and what it names the mass-shell condition is the four-speed budget of Proposition III.2. The Hamilton–Jacobi equation of a free relativistic particle and the McGucken constraint are one equation, related by the constant −mc. This places the constraint of Theorem VI.1 within a standard framework of classical mechanics while supplying the physical quantity — x₄'s advance — that the standard framework leaves as an abstract generating function.

Definition IV.4.2 (the free-sector density). The free sector of ℒ_McG is

ℒ_free = −(ρ c²/2) (∂_μX ∂^μX + 1) √−g, (IV.7)

with ρ(x) a real scalar field of dimension mass per unit volume. The field ρ enters as the Lagrange multiplier enforcing (C).

Proposition IV.3 (the three field equations of the free sector).

Variation of S_free = ∫ ℒ_free d⁴x with respect to ρ, X and g^μν gives, respectively:

(a) the constraint, ∂_μX ∂^μX = −1;

(b) the continuity equation, ∇_μ(ρ ∂^μX) = 0, equivalently ∇_μ(ρ u^μ) = 0 with u^μ = −c ∂^μX;

(c) the stress-energy tensor, evaluated on the constraint,

T^{(free)}_μν = ρ c² ∂_μX ∂_νX = ρ u_μ u_ν.

Proof. (a) ℒ_free is linear in ρ, so δS/δρ = 0 returns the bracket. (b) δS/δX = 0 gives ∂_μ(√−g ρ g^{μν} ∂_νX) = 0, which is ∇_μ(ρ ∂^μX) = 0; substituting u^μ = −c ∂^μX and absorbing the constant gives the stated form. (c) With T_μν ≡ −(2/√−g) δ(√−g ℒ_free)/δg^{μν}, and using δ(√−g)/δg^{μν} = −(1/2)√−g g_μν, one obtains T_μν = ρc² ∂_μX ∂_νX + g_μν ℒ_free. The bracket in ℒ_free vanishes on the constraint, so the second term drops and T_μν = ρc² ∂_μX ∂_νX = ρ u_μu_ν. ∎

Three consequences follow, each of which the earlier square-root form could not deliver.

The multiplier is a physical quantity. Equation (b) states that ρ u^μ is a conserved current, and equation (c) identifies ρ as the coefficient of u_μu_ν in the stress-energy tensor. The multiplier enforcing the McGucken constraint is therefore the rest-mass density of the congruence carried by x₄'s advance, and the mass parameter m of Proposition IV.1 is its worldline restriction. The McGucken reading: the four-speed budget is not imposed on the matter from outside — it is imposed by a field whose value is how much matter is present.

Geodesic motion is a theorem rather than an assumption. T^{(free)}_μν = ρ u_μu_ν is the stress-energy tensor of pressureless dust. Coupling ℒ_free to ℒ_grav and applying the contracted Bianchi identity ∇^μG_μν = 0 to the field equations of Proposition VI.3 gives ∇^μT^{(free)}_μν = 0, which with the continuity equation (b) reduces to u^μ∇_μ u_ν = 0. The integral curves of x₄'s advance are timelike geodesics. The equation of motion obtained in §IV.2 by varying the worldline action is recovered here as a consequence of the free sector's coupling to the gravitational sector, without a separate postulate of geodesic motion.

The foliation of Proposition III.6 is the level-set family of X. The lapse and shift of the ADM decomposition are fixed by X: the unit normal to Σ_{x₄} is n_μ = −∂_μX, the shift vanishes in the frame comoving with the congruence, and the lapse is the ratio of x₄-advance to coordinate time. The preferred x₄-foliation asserted in Proposition III.6 is therefore supplied by a field equation of ℒ_McG rather than adopted as a choice of slicing.

Remark IV.5 (relation to the dust formulations of the general-relativity literature). The density (IV.7) has the form of the velocity-potential representation of pressureless dust used as a material reference frame in canonical gravity — the Gaussian reference fluid of Kuchař and Torre [Kuchař-Torre 1991] and the dust action of Brown [Brown 1993], both of which introduce a scalar T with a unit-timelike gradient enforced by a multiplier that turns out to be the dust density. The McGucken reading of that literature: those constructions introduce a dust field in order to obtain a preferred time function, and the field is added to the theory for that purpose. Under the McGucken Principle the same field is already present, because x₄ advances at rate ic from every event whether or not a reference fluid is introduced, and its unit-timelike gradient is the four-speed budget of Proposition III.2 rather than a condition imposed on an auxiliary fluid. What the canonical-gravity literature adds by hand as a clock, ℒ_McG carries as the free sector of the Lagrangian of physics. The mathematical apparatus of those papers — the constraint algebra, the deparametrization, the conservation of the dust density — transfers directly and is available to the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework as established results.

#### IV.4.3 The worldline action as the restriction of the density

Everything below descends from the physical expansion of x₄. Proposition IV.1 established that −mc ∫_γ |dx₄| is the unique Poincaré-invariant, reparametrization-invariant, first-order local functional of a timelike worldline. The present section establishes that the density (IV.7) is that same functional written on ℳ, so that the uniqueness of Proposition IV.1 carries over to the field form and Theorem VI.1 inherits it.

Proposition IV.4 (einbein form and elimination of the multiplier).

Along a single worldline z^μ(λ), the restriction of the free-sector density to that curve is the einbein form

S_e[z, e] = (1/2) ∫ dλ [ e⁻¹ ẋ^μ ẋ_μ − e m²c² ],

with e(λ) the worldline restriction of the multiplier ρ. Varying e gives e = √(−ẋ^μẋ_μ) / (mc); substituting this value returns

S_e = −mc ∫ dλ √(−ẋ^μ ẋ_μ) = −mc ∫_γ |dx₄| = −mc² ∫_γ dτ,

which is the functional of Proposition IV.1.

Proof. The variation δS_e/δe = −(1/2)[e⁻² ẋ·ẋ + m²c²] = 0 gives e² = −ẋ·ẋ/(m²c²), and e > 0 for a timelike curve selects e = √(−ẋ·ẋ)/(mc). Substituting, the two terms contribute −(mc/2)√(−ẋ·ẋ) each, giving −mc∫√(−ẋ·ẋ) dλ. The three written forms are equal by Proposition III.1 and the definition dτ² = −ds²/c², as in §IV.1. ∎

Proposition IV.5 (equivalence of the field and worldline representations).

(i) Every integral curve of a solution X of the constraint (C) extremizes −mc ∫ |dx₄| among timelike curves with the same endpoints.

(ii) Conversely, every timelike geodesic of ℳ is an integral curve of some solution X of (C), on any region where the congruence containing it is free of caustics.

Proof. (i) is the geodesic result established after Proposition IV.3: the integral curves of ∂^μX are geodesics once ℒ_free is coupled to ℒ_grav, and timelike geodesics are the extremals of −mc∫|dx₄| by §IV.2. (ii) is the method of characteristics applied to the Hamilton–Jacobi equation of Remark IV.4: the characteristic curves of g^μν∂_μS∂_νS = −m²c² are the timelike geodesics, and given a geodesic congruence with a hypersurface transverse to it, integrating the arclength along the congruence produces a solution X on the region the congruence covers without self-intersection. ∎

The two propositions together establish what the free sector requires. The functional proved unique on the worldline in Proposition IV.1 is recovered from the density (IV.7) by eliminating the multiplier and restricting to one curve, so the density is a field-theoretic writing of that unique functional rather than a second and different choice. The uniqueness claim of Theorem VI.1 for the free sector is therefore a claim about the density, and it is established by Proposition IV.1 together with Propositions IV.4 and IV.5.

#### IV.4.4 Scope: caustics and the single-valuedness of X

Everything below descends from the physical expansion of x₄. The advance proceeds spherically symmetrically from every event, and the wavefronts it generates intersect. Where they do, the accumulated advance from a given initial hypersurface has more than one value at the same event, and X ceases to be a single-valued field.

The scope limit is stated directly. Definition IV.4.1 defines X on a region covered by the congruence without self-intersection. At a caustic — a point where neighbouring integral curves of the advance cross — the expansion scalar θ = ∇_μu^μ diverges, the solution of the Hamilton–Jacobi equation becomes multivalued, and (IV.7) describes the configuration only on each single-valued branch separately. This is the same phenomenon that appears in the Huygens-wavefront treatment of §VIII.14, where the caustic of x₄'s spherical wavefront is identified with the support of the retarded Green's function, and in the six-sense locality of the McGucken Sphere of §VIII.9, where the caustic sense is one of the six.

Two points bound the consequences. First, caustics of a dust congruence are generic, so the field representation of the free sector is local rather than global, and Theorem VI.1 is a statement about ℒ_McG on caustic-free regions. Second, the matter, gauge and gravitational sectors are unaffected: their densities are polynomial in the fields and their derivatives and require no single-valued potential. The limitation belongs to the representation of the free sector by a potential X, and the physics it represents — the advance of x₄ at rate ic from every event, including events on a caustic — is unaffected, since dx₄/dt = ic is a statement about the rate of the manifold's expansion and holds at a caustic exactly as it holds elsewhere. The McGucken reading: caustics limit the chart, not the Principle.

#### IV.4.5 Reconciliation with the cosmological constraint term of §VIII.12.2

Everything below descends from the physical expansion of x₄. Section VIII.12.2 introduces the McGucken action with an x₄-constraint carried by a multiplier, written there as S = ∫d⁴x √g [ (c⁴/16πG)R + λ(g₄₄ + c²) + ℒ_matter ], and uses it to argue that the constraint contributes at Hubble scale. That multiplier and the multiplier ρ of (IV.7) are the same object, and the relation between the two constraint forms is the following.

The condition ∂_μX ∂^μX = −1 is covariant: it holds in every coordinate system. The condition g₄₄ = −c² is a gauge condition: it holds in the coordinate system in which X itself is the time coordinate. Adopting the x₄-gauge — taking x⁰ = X and using the foliation of Proposition III.6 with vanishing shift — gives ∂_μX = δ_μ⁰, whereupon ∂_μX ∂^μX = g^{00}, and the covariant constraint becomes g^{00} = −1, equivalently g₀₀ = −1 in length coordinates and g₄₄ = −c² in the coordinate-time form of §VIII.12.2. The two constraints are one constraint written in two ways, and the two multipliers are one multiplier: by Proposition IV.3(c), it is the rest-mass density ρ of the congruence.

This identification sharpens the argument of §VIII.12.2 in one respect and corrects it in another.

It sharpens the argument by supplying the physical meaning of the multiplier. Section VIII.12.2 describes λ as enforcing that x₄ advances at rate c and observes that λ → 0 on scales well inside the Hubble radius. Proposition IV.3 identifies λ as a mass density, so the statement that it becomes negligible on small scales is the statement that the x₄-congruence carries a small rest-mass density there compared with the local matter and gauge sectors — a quantitative claim about a physical quantity rather than about an auxiliary variable.

It corrects the argument in the equation-of-state assignment. The stress-energy tensor the constraint contributes is T_μν = ρ u_μu_ν by Proposition IV.3(c), which is pressureless dust with equation of state w = 0. A cosmological constant has w = −1. The constraint term of §VIII.12.2 therefore does not by itself supply Λ, and the derivation of Λ in Conjecture VIII.2.4 rests on the asymptotic-horizon and holographic-cutoff route of §VIII.12.2 rather than on the multiplier term. Conjecture VIII.2.4 therefore stands on the asymptotic-horizon grounds stated there, and the multiplier term contributes to the matter budget rather than to the vacuum term.

The corrected assignment has a use. A pressureless, geodesic, conserved component that couples to gravity and to nothing else is the phenomenology that §VIII.21.7 attributes to the geometric mis-accounting underlying observed galactic rotation curves and lensing. The dust component of the x₄-advance field is the natural carrier of that phenomenology within ℒ_McG: it is present in the Lagrangian by the same Principle that forces the other four sectors, it is pressureless and collisionless by Proposition IV.3(c), it follows geodesics by the Bianchi consequence established above, and its density ρ is fixed by the continuity equation (b) rather than by a free profile. Whether the profile so fixed reproduces the flat rotation curves and the Tully-Fisher relation quantitatively is a specific open computation — the density ρ must be solved for on a galactic-scale metric using (a), (b) and Proposition VI.3 jointly — and it is a computation the field form of the free sector makes well-posed for the first time. Under the square-root form of the free sector this question could not be posed, because a constant density has no profile to solve for.

## V. The Matter Sector and Its Uniqueness

This section derives the Lagrangian for quantum matter under the McGucken Principle and proves its uniqueness. The free-particle action of §IV governs classical worldlines; the quantum matter Lagrangian governs matter fields — complex-valued, or spinor-valued, fields on spacetime — under the Compton-frequency coupling (Proposition III.4) and the matter orientation condition (M).

### V.1 The Matter Field and the Matter Orientation Condition

A quantum matter field Ψ on Minkowski spacetime is, under the McGucken Principle, a standing wave in x₄ at the Compton frequency of the particle species. By [MG-Dirac, §IV], Ψ takes values in the even-grade subalgebra of the spacetime Clifford algebra Cl(3,1), with the matter orientation condition

Ψ(x, x₄) = Ψ₀(x) · exp(+I · k_C · x₄), k_C = mc/ℏ > 0,

where I is the Clifford pseudoscalar of spacetime algebra and k_C is the Compton wavenumber of the particle species. The positive sign of k_C selects matter; antimatter is described by the opposite sign. The factor exp(+I·k_C·x₄) is the physical rest-mass phase of the particle, elevated from the mathematical global phase of standard quantum field theory to a physical oscillation driven by x₄’s advance (cf. [MG-deBroglie, §IV.3] for the de Broglie-relation derivation that makes this explicit).

In four-component spinor notation, with γ^μ the Dirac matrices satisfying {γ^μ, γ^ν} = 2η^μν, the matter field Ψ becomes a Dirac spinor ψ, and the matter orientation condition translates to the positive-energy mass-shell condition (iγ^μ∂_μ − m)ψ = 0 — the Dirac equation — as shown in [MG-Dirac, §V]. The Lagrangian derivation in this section is most cleanly presented in the Dirac-spinor notation; the Clifford-algebraic form gives the same result.

### V.2 The Dirac Lagrangian

The Dirac Lagrangian for a free matter field of mass m is

ℒ_Dirac = ψ̄(iγ^μ ∂_μ − m)ψ,

with ψ̄ = ψ†γ⁰ the Dirac conjugate. Varying the corresponding action S_Dirac = ∫ℒ_Dirac d⁴x with respect to ψ̄ gives the Dirac equation (iγ^μ∂_μ − m)ψ = 0 directly. Under the McGucken Principle, this Lagrangian has a specific derivational origin:

(a) The requirement that the Lagrangian be Lorentz-invariant forces ℒ to be a Lorentz scalar built from ψ, ψ̄, ∂_μψ, ∂_μψ̄, and the Minkowski metric η_μν together with the Dirac matrices γ^μ.

(b) The requirement that the field equations be first-order in derivatives (the relativistic-covariance requirement that anticipates Dirac’s 1928 derivation) forces ℒ to be linear in first derivatives of ψ and ψ̄.

(c) The requirement that the field equations reproduce the Klein-Gordon dispersion relation E² = p²c² + m²c⁴ (mass-shell condition) forces the coefficient of the kinetic term to involve γ^μ∂_μ with {γ^μ, γ^ν} = 2η^μν.

(d) The requirement that the Lagrangian reduce, on-shell, to the free-particle action −mc²∫dτ of Proposition IV.1 (the classical-limit correspondence) fixes the overall normalization.

These four requirements together force ℒ_Dirac to the form ψ̄(iγ^μ∂_μ − m)ψ up to an overall real multiplicative constant and additive total derivatives. The derivation is carried out in detail in [MG-Dirac, §III], where it is shown that the Clifford algebra {γ^μ, γ^ν} = 2η^μν is itself a theorem of the McGucken Principle (the Minkowski signature η = diag(+,+,+,−) comes from dx₄/dt = ic, and the Clifford algebra is the minimal algebra supporting first-order Lorentz-covariant spinor fields on this signature).

### V.3 The Uniqueness Theorem for the Matter Sector

Proposition V.1 (Uniqueness of the Dirac matter sector).

Let ψ be a spinor field on Minkowski spacetime satisfying the matter orientation condition Ψ(x, x₄) = Ψ₀(x)·exp(+I·k_C·x₄) with k_C = mc/ℏ > 0. Let ℒ_matter be a real scalar Lagrangian density for ψ satisfying: (a) Lorentz invariance including parity — ℒ_matter transforms as a true scalar (not a pseudoscalar) under the full Lorentz group O(3,1) including the parity inversion P: x → (−x, t); (b) hermiticity — ℒ_matter is real, i.e., ℒ_matter = ℒ_matter; (c) first-order in derivatives — ℒ_matter depends on ∂_μψ and ∂_μψ̄ but not on higher derivatives; (d) one matter species — ℒ_matter is a functional of a single spinor field ψ (not of two or more independent fields); (e) locality at a point — ℒ_matter(x) depends only on ψ, ψ̄, and their first derivatives evaluated at x, not on values at other points; (f) mass-shell consistency — the field equations derived from ℒ_matter, applied to the rest-mass phase e^(−imc²τ/ℏ) of ψ, reproduce the Klein-Gordon dispersion relation E² = p²c² + m²c⁴. Under these six conditions, the unique ℒ_matter (up to overall real constant, field redefinition ψ → Aψ for nonzero real A, and additive total derivatives) is*

ℒ_matter = ψ̄(iγ^μ ∂_μ − m)ψ,

the Dirac Lagrangian.

Proof.

The argument follows Dirac’s 1928 derivation, adapted here to the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework and with the conditions made explicit. By condition (a) (Lorentz invariance including parity) and condition (c) (first-order derivatives), ℒ_matter must be a Lorentz scalar (not pseudoscalar) built from ψ, ψ̄, ∂_μψ, ∂_μψ̄, and the 16 linearly independent elements of the Dirac algebra: 1, γ^μ, σ^{μν} = (i/2)[γ^μ, γ^ν], γ^μγ⁵, γ⁵. The parity-scalar bilinears in (ψ, ψ̄) with at most one derivative are: ψ̄ψ (scalar, zero-derivative), ψ̄γ⁵ψ (pseudoscalar, zero-derivative), ψ̄γ^μ∂_μψ (scalar, one-derivative, after contraction of γ^μ with ∂_μ), ψ̄γ^μγ⁵∂_μψ (pseudoscalar, one-derivative), ψ̄σ^{μν}∂_μψ (scalar, but antisymmetric in (μ,ν) so the derivative piece is a curl term), plus the corresponding terms with ∂_μ acting on ψ̄. Condition (a)’s parity requirement excludes the pseudoscalar terms ψ̄γ⁵ψ and ψ̄γ^μγ⁵∂_μψ (both of which reverse sign under parity because γ⁵ → −γ⁵ under P). The σ^{μν}∂_μψ term is antisymmetric under (μ ↔ ν) on the σ side but symmetric on the ∂_μ∂_ν side (when acted on by a second derivative), so ∫ψ̄σ^{μν}∂_μψ d⁴x reduces by integration by parts either to a total derivative (vanishing with standard boundary conditions) or to an equivalent of the γ^μ∂_μ term after using {γ^μ, γ^ν} = 2η^μν. No Pauli-term coupling survives in the free sector. The surviving parity-scalar Lagrangian is therefore

ℒ_matter = α · ψ̄(iγ^μ∂_μ)ψ + α’ · (i∂_μψ̄)γ^μψ + γ · ψ̄ψ,

with real coefficients α, α’, γ (by condition (b), hermiticity). By condition (d) (one matter species) the only field variables are ψ and ψ̄ (not additional fields ψ’, ψ”). By condition (e) (locality at a point) all three surviving terms are already local, so locality imposes no further constraint at this stage — it has been used implicitly by restricting to terms evaluated at x. Hermiticity (condition (b)) further relates α and α’: the combination α·ψ̄(iγ^μ∂_μ)ψ + α’·(i∂_μψ̄)γ^μψ is real if and only if α = α’, because ψ̄(iγ^μ∂_μ)ψ and its complex conjugate [ψ̄(iγ^μ∂_μ)ψ]* = (i∂_μψ̄)γ^μψ (modulo total derivative, using (γ^μ)† = γ⁰γ^μγ⁰) combine symmetrically into α[ψ̄(iγ^μ∂_μ)ψ + (i∂_μψ̄)γ^μψ]/2 = α·ψ̄(iγ^μ∂_μ)ψ up to a total derivative (integration by parts on the second term). Therefore modulo total derivatives, the Lagrangian reduces to

ℒ_matter = α · ψ̄(iγ^μ∂_μ)ψ + γ · ψ̄ψ.

By condition (f) (mass-shell consistency), varying the action ∫ℒ_matter d⁴x with respect to ψ̄ gives the Euler-Lagrange equation α(iγ^μ∂_μ)ψ + γ ψ = 0, which must be equivalent to the Dirac equation (iγ^μ∂_μ − m)ψ = 0 whose squaring (using {γ^μ, γ^ν} = 2η^μν) produces (∂_μ∂^μ + m²/ℏ²·c²)ψ = 0 — the Klein-Gordon equation with the correct mass m set by the Compton coupling of the matter orientation condition (M). Matching coefficients: α(iγ^μ∂_μ) + γ = iγ^μ∂_μ − m requires α = 1 and γ = −m up to an overall real scale. The overall scale is the field redefinition ψ → Aψ (real A ≠ 0), which rescales both α and γ by A² while leaving the equations of motion unchanged; this is the field-redefinition freedom named in the theorem statement. Setting the normalization to α = 1, we obtain

ℒ_matter = ψ̄(iγ^μ∂_μ − m)ψ,

which is the Dirac Lagrangian. The uniqueness is therefore (i) up to overall real constant (absorbed into the field redefinition), (ii) up to field redefinition ψ → Aψ with real A ≠ 0 (which does not change physics), and (iii) up to additive total derivatives (which do not affect equations of motion or the action under standard boundary conditions). All three equivalences are trivial in the sense that they produce the same physical theory. No physically inequivalent alternative satisfies the six conditions.

∎

#### Remark V.1.0 (On condition (a)’s parity requirement and the weak sector)

Condition (a) — that ℒ_matter be a parity-scalar, not a pseudoscalar — is a nontrivial physical input. The free-matter Dirac Lagrangian ψ̄(iγ^μ∂_μ − m)ψ satisfies parity, as does the full QED sector. The weak sector of the Standard Model, however, does not: the weak interaction violates parity maximally through its V−A structure (Wu 1957, Lee-Yang 1957), and the Standard Model’s Lagrangian contains genuine pseudoscalar terms (e.g. the chiral projections P_L = (1 − γ⁵)/2 applied to fermions coupling to the W boson). Proposition V.1 therefore establishes the uniqueness of the Dirac matter sector — the kinetic and mass structure common to all fermions — not the uniqueness of the full weak-gauge coupling, which enters through the SU(2)_L gauge-covariant derivative D_μ in §VI. The decomposition is standard: the Dirac Lagrangian of this section is the parity-symmetric free-matter sector, and parity-violating weak couplings enter only through the gauge-field sector (Propositions VI.2-VI.3) via the chiral SU(2)_L × U(1)_Y structure. The full Standard Model Lagrangian is the sum of parity-symmetric free-matter sectors (this section) plus parity-violating gauge couplings (next section), with the parity-violation entirely confined to the gauge-interaction structure. An alternative phrasing: condition (a) can be weakened to ‘Lorentz invariance without parity’ at the cost of enlarging the allowed Lagrangian space to include γ⁵ and γ^μγ⁵ terms, which must then be restricted at the interaction-coupling stage by the V−A structure of the weak gauge sector. The present paper takes the Dirac kinetic/mass sector as parity-symmetric by condition (a), following the standard textbook convention of Peskin-Schroeder §3.3 and Weinberg QFT vol. I §5.5.

#### Remark V.1 (The matter orientation condition (M) as rigorous algebraic constraint)

The matter orientation condition (M) — Ψ(x, x₄) = Ψ₀(x)·exp(+I·k_C·x₄) with k_C = mc/ℏ > 0 — was used implicitly in condition (e) (mass-shell consistency): the rest-mass phase of ψ at the Compton frequency is what fixes the mass parameter m in ℒ_Dirac. Without the matter orientation condition, the value of m would be a free parameter of the Lagrangian; with it, m is fixed by the Compton coupling of matter to x₄’s advance, with one m per particle species. The matter orientation condition is therefore a input to the uniqueness theorem, and its derivation from the McGucken Principle in [MG-Dirac, §IV] is what makes the Dirac Lagrangian a consequence of dx₄/dt = ic rather than an independent postulate.

The comprehensive April 19 Dirac paper [MG-Dirac, §IV.2] establishes (M) as a rigorous algebraic constraint on even-grade multivectors in Cl(1,3) rather than a pictorial claim. An even-grade multivector Ψ satisfies the matter orientation condition at Compton frequency k > 0 if and only if there exist an even-grade rest-frame amplitude Ψ₀ and a real scalar x₄ such that Ψ(x, x₄) = Ψ₀(x)·exp(+I·kx₄) with multiplication performed on the right. Three features distinguish this from earlier pictorial formulations: (i) the sign of k is positive — this is what distinguishes matter from antimatter; (ii) the x₄-dependence enters through right-multiplication — this is what picks out a preferred side of the bivector action on Ψ; (iii) the pseudoscalar I, not an abstract imaginary unit, is the generator — tying the phase structure to the 4D Clifford geometry. Condition (M) is therefore an algebraic constraint, not a geometric picture, and Theorem IV.3 of [MG-Dirac] operates on this algebraic structure directly.

#### Remark V.2 (The half-angle as theorem: single-sided uniqueness)

The Dirac Lagrangian ψ̄(iγ^μ∂_μ − m)ψ derived in Proposition V.1 governs spinor fields that transform under rotations with the characteristic half-angle: under a spatial rotation by angle θ about axis n̂, ψ transforms as ψ → exp(−iθ/2·n̂·σ)ψ, and a full 2π rotation takes ψ → −ψ rather than ψ → ψ. This half-angle is not a representation-theoretic convention but a theorem of the matter orientation condition (M). Theorem IV.3 of [MG-Dirac] establishes that for any rotor R = exp(θ/2·e_P) generated by a spatial bivector e_P, left-action Ψ → RΨ preserves (M) across all Lorentz generators, while sandwich action Ψ → R⁻¹ΨR does not preserve (M) for x₄-involving bivectors — sandwich action generates a transformed pseudoscalar R⁻¹IR ≠ I that introduces an antimatter admixture into a would-be matter field. Single-sided transformation is therefore the unique action preserving (M), and the half-angle arises because single-sided action applies only one factor of the rotor to Ψ rather than the two factors of sandwich action.

This replaces the earlier pictorial arguments about matter’s x₄-orientation with a rigorous algebraic theorem, and explains why nature chose spin-½ as a geometric necessity: matter fields, by satisfying (M), must transform by single-sided action to preserve their defining orientation constraint, and single-sided action forces the half-angle. The SU(2) → SO(3) double cover is the direct geometric consequence: two distinct spinor transformations (at θ and θ + 2π) correspond to the same vector rotation, because vectors do not satisfy (M) and see the full angle via sandwich action R⁻¹vR.

#### Remark V.3 (Antimatter and charge conjugation: Doran-Lasenby component verification)

The uniqueness theorem V.1 produces the Dirac Lagrangian ψ̄(iγ^μ∂_μ − m)ψ, which — as Dirac 1931 showed — has both positive-energy (matter) and negative-energy (antimatter) solutions. In the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework, the matter/antimatter distinction is the sign of k_C in (M): +k_C for matter, −k_C for antimatter. [MG-Dirac, §VIII] closes the identification at the component level via the Doran-Lasenby correspondence ψ_matrix ↔ Ψ_geometric·ξ₀ between 4-component matrix spinors and even-grade multivectors in Cl(1,3) with reference spinor ξ₀ = u₊ = (1,0,1,0)^T/√2. Starting from a rest-frame spin-up electron with ψ_e(t) = u₊·e^(−imc²t/ℏ) corresponding to Ψ_e(t) = exp(−γ²γ¹·mc²t/ℏ), the standard matrix operation C γ⁰ ψ* applied to ψ_e produces (0,−1,0,1)^T·e^(+imc²t/ℏ) — the rest-frame spin-up positron. The geometric operation Ψ_e → Ψ_e·γ₂γ₁ (right-multiplication by the bivector γ₂γ₁) produces the same 4-spinor component by component. This is the explicit verification that charge conjugation IS geometric right-action reversing x₄-orientation, not merely analogous to it.

Two additional downstream results of §VIII of [MG-Dirac] strengthen the Dirac-sector treatment of ℒ_McG. First, the identity γ⁴ = iγ⁰ is derived rather than posited: the Clifford algebra must be consistent across both LTD-native coordinates (x₁,x₂,x₃,x₄) with Euclidean-looking signature (γ⁴)² = +1 and Lorentzian coordinates (x⁰,x¹,x²,x³) with (γ⁰)² = −1, and the LTD coordinate relation x₄ = ix⁰ forces γ⁴ = iγ⁰ as the unique relation consistent with both. Second, the Yvon-Takabayashi angle β in the Hestenes decomposition Ψ = ρ^(1/2)·R·exp(Iβ/2) acquires a physical interpretation: β measures the local tilt between the particle’s x₄-phase frame and the universal x₄-expansion direction, with β = 0 corresponding to pure matter (satisfying (M) with k > 0) and β = π to pure antimatter. CPT emerges automatically as full 4D coordinate inversion preserving the x₄-dynamics, not as a separately-proved theorem requiring Lorentz invariance and locality as independent axioms.

#### Remark V.4 (Unified T-violation at all scales from the same dx₄/dt = +ic source)

The sign of dx₄/dt = +ic (not −ic) is the geometric source of T-violation at every scale — from microscopic CP/T-violation in kaon and B-meson oscillations to the macroscopic thermodynamic arrow of time. [MG-Dirac, §X.4] establishes this unification by deriving both endpoints from the same source through two parallel chains. The microscopic chain [§X.4.3]: the positive sign of dx₄/dt fixes k_f = m_f c/ℏ > 0 for every fermion species; SU(2)_L weak mixing among quarks of different masses produces Compton-frequency interference in |ψ_weak(x₄)|² = |Σᵢ U_ij·exp(+I·kᵢx₄)·|mᵢ⟩|²; with three generations this interference has an irreducible complex phase (the CKM phase δ, the Kobayashi-Maskawa three-generation requirement now a geometric theorem); and by CPT exactness (automatic in the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework), CP-violation implies T-violation — directly observed in CPLEAR 1998 and BaBar 2012. The macroscopic chain [§X.4.4]: matter is advected by x₄-expansion at rate c isotropically; the central limit theorem gives Gaussian spreading dS/dt = (3/2)k_B/t > 0, with the positive sign inherited from the positive sign of dx₄/dt.

Both chains start from the same source and reach their endpoints through specific derivations. A counterfactual universe with dx₄/dt = −ic would simultaneously be an antimatter universe (every k_f negative), a time-reversed universe (dS/dt < 0), and a universe in which kaons oscillate with the opposite asymmetry — all three features flipping together because they share a single geometric source. In the Standard Model, microscopic T-violation (CKM phase as free parameter) and macroscopic irreversibility (Past Hypothesis as independent boundary condition) are two separate phenomena with two separate explanations; no Standard Model mechanism links them. In the McGucken Lagrangian framework, they are locked together as consequences of the single geometric fact that dx₄/dt has a definite positive sign. The same ℒ_McG whose matter sector is fixed by Proposition V.1 also fixes, through its derivation chain from dx₄/dt = +ic, the sign of T-violation at every scale — strong CP conservation (no θ-term) because x₄’s expansion acts symmetrically on x₁x₂x₃; weak CP violation with a geometric origin in Compton-frequency interference; and the thermodynamic arrow inherited from the same expansion direction. This is the concrete content of the claim that the four-fold uniqueness theorem (Theorem VI.1) captures the physics of the Standard Model plus general relativity: the same single principle that forces ℒ_McG also forces the sign of T-violation at every scale at which it appears.

## VI. The Gauge and Gravitational Sectors, and the Full Uniqueness Theorem

This section derives the gauge and gravitational sectors of the McGucken Lagrangian, proves the uniqueness of each, and states the full four-fold uniqueness theorem.

### VI.1 The Gauge Sector

By Proposition III.5, local x₄-phase invariance forces a U(1) gauge structure with covariant derivative D_μ = ∂_μ − ieA_μ. The matter Lagrangian of §V, modified to be locally x₄-phase-invariant, becomes

ℒ_matter+gauge = ψ̄(iγ^μ D_μ − m)ψ = ψ̄(iγ^μ ∂_μ − m)ψ + e ψ̄γ^μψ A_μ,

with the second term the minimal coupling of matter to the gauge field. The gauge field itself requires a kinetic term of its own, since otherwise the field equations δS/δA_μ = 0 would be purely algebraic and would not permit the dynamical gauge field that electromagnetism requires. The natural gauge-invariant kinetic term is

ℒ_gauge = −¼ F_μν F^μν, F_μν = ∂_μ A_ν − ∂_ν A_μ,

which is the Maxwell Lagrangian. The combined matter-plus-gauge Lagrangian is

ℒ_matter+gauge+Maxwell = ψ̄(iγ^μ D_μ − m)ψ − ¼ F_μν F^μν,

which is the QED Lagrangian [MG-QED]. The extension to non-Abelian gauge groups — the Yang-Mills structure — proceeds by replacing ∂_μ − ieA_μ with ∂_μ − igA^a_μ T^a (with T^a the generators of the gauge Lie algebra) and F_μν with F^a_μν = ∂_μA^a_ν − ∂_νA^a_μ + g f^{abc} A^b_μ A^c_ν, per [MG-SM, Theorem 10]. The general Yang-Mills Lagrangian for any compact Lie group G is then uniquely −¼ Tr(F_μνF^μν) by [MG-SM, Theorem 11]. The specific Standard Model gauge group U(1)_Y × SU(2)_L × SU(3)_c is fixed by a separate argument, given in [MG-QFT, §9]. The papers [MG-Noether] and [MG-Broken] propose geometric identifications of each factor with x₄-perpendicular sectors (U(1)_Y with overall phase, SU(2)_L with the Spin(4) stabilizer subgroup, SU(3)_c with spatial-triple rotations) as the natural geometric reading of the empirically observed group, but the full derivation of the specific group from the Principle remains open work.

Proposition VI.2 (Uniqueness of the Yang-Mills gauge sector).

Let G be a Lie group with Lie algebra g, let ψ be a matter field transforming in a representation R of G, and let A^a_μ be the gauge connection for local G-invariance. Let ℒ_gauge be a real scalar Lagrangian density satisfying: (a) local G-invariance — ℒ_gauge is invariant under local G transformations of ψ and A^a_μ; (b) Lorentz invariance; (c) first-order in gauge-field derivatives — ℒ_gauge depends on ∂_μA^a_ν but not on higher derivatives; (d) renormalizability — ℒ_gauge has mass dimension ≤ 4 in natural units; (e) parity invariance — ℒ_gauge is invariant under P. Under these five conditions, the unique ℒ_gauge is

ℒ_gauge = −¼ F^a_μν F^{aμν}, F^a_μν = ∂_μ A^a_ν − ∂_ν A^a_μ + g f^{abc} A^b_μ A^c_ν,

the Yang-Mills Lagrangian, up to a θ-term (parity-odd topological term) which is excluded by condition (e).

Proof.

The proof follows the standard Yang-Mills uniqueness result. By condition (a), ℒ_gauge must be invariant under A^a_μ → A^a_μ + ∂_μα^a + f^{abc} A^b_μ α^c (local gauge transformation). The gauge-invariant combinations of A^a_μ built from at most first derivatives are: (i) F^a_μν itself (the non-Abelian field strength); (ii) its dual F̃^a_μν = ½ε_{μνρσ}F^{aρσ}. Higher gauge-covariant derivatives such as D_μF^a_νρ are excluded by condition (c), which restricts the Lagrangian to terms built from F^a_μν without further differentiation. Higher-order invariants in F (products like Tr(F³) built using the invariant symbol d^{abc} on the gauge indices) are dimensionally dim ≥ 6 in natural units and excluded by condition (d). The most general Lorentz-scalar quadratic functional of F^a_μν under conditions (a), (b), (c), (d) is

ℒ_gauge = α F^a_μν F^{aμν} + β F^a_μν F̃^{aμν}

with α, β real constants (trace structure on the gauge Lie algebra is fixed to the Killing form δ^{ab} for a simple compact Lie algebra; for semisimple algebras each simple factor has its own coefficient α, but the argument is unchanged). The F·F̃ term (the θ-term) is parity-odd — under P the field-strength components transform as F^a_{ij} → F^a_{ij} and F^a_{0i} → −F^a_{0i}, so F^aF^a is parity-even while F^aF̃^a (which contracts electric and magnetic parts) is parity-odd. By condition (e), β = 0, excluding the θ-term. By condition (d) (renormalizability, mass dimension ≤ 4), F^a_μν has mass dimension 2 and F^a_μνF^{aμν} has dimension 4, so the coefficient α is dimensionless. Fixing the conventional normalization α = −1/4 (to match the Maxwell Lagrangian in the Abelian U(1) case and to give the canonical-form Yang-Mills field equations D_μF^{aμν} = 0) gives

ℒ_gauge = −¼ F^a_μν F^{aμν},

which is the claimed Yang-Mills Lagrangian. In the Abelian case G = U(1), f^{abc} = 0 and this reduces to the Maxwell Lagrangian −¼F_μνF^μν. Remark on scope: condition (e) constrains the pure gauge kinetic term −¼F^aF^a to be parity-even. It does not constrain how matter couples to gauge fields (which is specified by the gauge-covariant derivative D_μ = ∂_μ − ig A^a_μ T^a in the matter Lagrangian ℒ_matter of Proposition V.1). The chiral structure of the Standard Model’s weak sector — where SU(2)_L couples only to left-chiral matter fields — enters through the matter-sector representation choice, not through the pure gauge kinetic term. Proposition VI.2 is therefore consistent with the Standard Model’s parity-violating weak interactions.

∎

#### Remark VI.1 (The θ-term and the strong CP problem)

The parity-odd term F·F̃ that Proposition VI.2 excludes — the so-called θ-term — is a topological invariant (a total derivative in the Abelian case, a Chern-Simons-like structure in the non-Abelian case) that does not contribute to the classical equations of motion but can contribute to quantum effects. Its coefficient in the QCD sector is the θ parameter, whose observed smallness (|θ_QCD| < 10⁻¹⁰) is the content of the strong CP problem. In the present paper’s Proposition VI.2, the θ-term is excluded at the Lagrangian level by the parity invariance requirement (condition (e)), providing a Lagrangian-level resolution: θ = 0 is forced by the parity-invariance requirement of the pure gauge kinetic term. A distinct and complementary argument is developed in [MG-Broken, §X]: the SU(3)_c sector arises from the three spatial dimensions, all of which are equivalent under x₄’s expansion (x₄’s expansion distinguishes SU(2)_L from SU(2)_R in the weak sector but does not distinguish among x₁, x₂, x₃), so no geometric mechanism exists to generate a strong CP-violating phase in the first place. The two arguments — Lagrangian-level parity invariance (present paper) and absence of a generating mechanism ([MG-Broken]) — are consistent and mutually reinforcing.

### VI.2 The Gravitational Sector

In curved spacetime, the McGucken Principle generalizes via the ADM foliation (Proposition III.6): x₄’s advance along a worldline is modulated by the local spatial curvature, and the Einstein field equations arise as the dynamical consistency condition of this modulation. The corresponding gravitational Lagrangian is the Einstein-Hilbert Lagrangian

ℒ_grav = (c⁴/16πG) R √−g,

with R the Ricci scalar of the spacetime metric g_μν and g = det(g_μν). By the standard variational derivation, ∫ℒ_grav d⁴x with respect to g_μν gives Einstein’s vacuum field equations G_μν = R_μν − ½Rg_μν = 0; coupled to the matter and gauge sectors, the combined Lagrangian gives G_μν = (8πG/c⁴) T_μν with T_μν the total stress-energy tensor.

Proposition VI.3 (Uniqueness of the Einstein-Hilbert gravitational sector (Lovelock’s theorem)).

Let g_μν be a Lorentzian metric on a four-dimensional spacetime manifold ℳ, and let ℒ_grav be a real scalar Lagrangian density satisfying: (a) diffeomorphism invariance — ℒ_grav is invariant under diffeomorphisms of ℳ; (b) locality — ℒ_grav is a local functional of g_μν and its derivatives at a spacetime point; (c) second-order field equations — the Euler-Lagrange equations derived from ℒ_grav are at most second-order in derivatives of g_μν. Under these three conditions, the unique (up to overall constant and a cosmological-constant term) ℒ_grav on a four-dimensional manifold is

ℒ_grav = α (R − 2Λ) √−g,

with R the Ricci scalar, Λ the cosmological constant, and α an overall real constant fixed by dimensional analysis to α = c⁴/(16πG). This is the Einstein-Hilbert Lagrangian with cosmological constant.

Proof.

This is Lovelock’s theorem (1971), applied here to the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework with the specific identification that α = c⁴/(16πG) is fixed by the McGucken Principle through the Newtonian-limit correspondence. The physical motivation for condition (c) — second-order field equations — is well-posedness of the Cauchy problem: Ostrogradsky’s theorem (1850) establishes that Lagrangians with non-degenerate higher-derivative dependencies produce Hamiltonians unbounded below, so a fundamental Lagrangian with second-order field equations is the unique choice avoiding ghost modes at the classical level.
