The proof of Lovelock’s theorem is standard: in four spacetime dimensions, the most general symmetric divergence-free rank-2 tensor E_μν constructed from the metric and its first two derivatives — equivalently, the variational derivative δS/δg^μν of a diffeomorphism-invariant action S — is a linear combination of the Einstein tensor G_μν = R_μν − ½Rg_μν and the metric g_μν itself (giving a cosmological-constant contribution). In 4D, no other such tensors exist: the Gauss-Bonnet tensor G_GB,μν built from R² − 4R_μν R^μν + R_μναβ R^μναβ is identically zero in four dimensions (by the Gauss-Bonnet identity), so the Gauss-Bonnet term is topological — its contribution to the action is proportional to the Euler characteristic of the manifold, independent of the metric, and therefore does not affect the field equations. The same holds for all higher Lovelock tensors in 4D. Integrating backward from the field-equation level to the Lagrangian, the most general Lagrangian satisfying conditions (a)-(c) in four dimensions is therefore ℒ_grav = α R√−g + β √−g (plus a boundary Gauss-Bonnet term that is topologically invariant and does not affect the field equations), which with β = −2αΛ gives the Einstein-Hilbert Lagrangian with cosmological constant.

The value α = c⁴/(16πG) is fixed by requiring that the Einstein field equations G_μν = (8πG/c⁴) T_μν reduce, in the Newtonian limit, to Poisson’s equation ∇²Φ = 4πGρ. In the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework, the Newtonian limit is derived in [MG-GR, §II.3 and Appendix A.1] as the non-relativistic regime of x₄’s advance coupled to a slowly-varying spatial metric, and the identification of G with the gravitational coupling constant is fixed by the matching of the geodesic equation to Newton’s law of universal gravitation. The full derivation of Newton’s law F = −GMm·r̂/r² from dx₄/dt = ic is supplied in [MG-Newton] through an eight-step derivation chain: (i) the master equation u^μ·u_μ = −c² from the McGucken Principle, (ii) the weak-field Schwarzschild metric ds² = −(1 − 2GM/rc²)c²dt² + (1 + 2GM/rc²)d x ² with mass stretching the three spatial dimensions while x₄’s expansion rate remains invariantly ic, (iii) the resulting gradient of clock rates dτ/dt ≈ 1 + Φ/c² with Φ = −GM/r derived from the invariant x₄-expansion meeting stretched spatial geometry, (iv) the Principle of Least Action (itself a theorem of dx₄/dt = ic per [MG-HLA]) requiring free particles to follow geodesics of maximal proper time, (v) the geodesic equation in the weak field reducing to d² r /dt² = −∇Φ = −GM·r̂/r² [MG-Newton, §V.2], (vi) spherical symmetry of x₄’s expansion generating the McGucken Sphere with area A(r) = 4πr² [MG-Newton, §VI.1], (vii) Gauss’s theorem applied to the McGucken Sphere giving | g |·4πr² = 4πGM hence | g | = GM/r² [MG-Newton, §VI.2], and (viii) the geometric origin of the 1/r² dependence itself: the surface area of a sphere in n-dimensional space scales as r^(n−1), and because there are exactly three spatial dimensions perpendicular to x₄, the boundary sphere is two-dimensional with area 4πr² and flux per unit area therefore falls as 1/r² [MG-Newton, §VI.3]. The inverse-square law is not a brute empirical fact fit to observation but a geometric theorem of the dimensionality of the space perpendicular to x₄ — the same reasoning that produces Coulomb’s law for charges and the 1/r² fall-off of light from a point source. The Einstein-Hilbert coupling α = c⁴/(16πG) is therefore not matched to Newton’s law from outside; it is determined by the same geometric principle that generates Newton’s law from within: the spherical symmetry of x₄’s isotropic expansion combined with Gauss’s theorem on the McGucken Sphere.

∎

#### Remark VI.2 (The cosmological constant in the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework)

The cosmological constant Λ appears in Proposition VI.3 as an additional term permitted by Lovelock uniqueness. In standard general relativity, Λ is a free parameter whose value is fixed empirically; the observed value Λ_obs ≈ 10⁻⁵² m⁻² is the source of the cosmological-constant problem (why is Λ so small compared to naive quantum-field-theoretic estimates?). In the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework, Λ is not a free parameter but is derived geometrically from the McGucken horizon structure. The dark-energy equation-of-state paper [MG-Lambda] derives w_eff(z) = −1 + Ω_m(z)/(6π) with no adjustable parameters, and the FRW holographic paper [MG-FRW-Holography] identifies Λ ∼ 1/R₄² with R₄ the McGucken radius at the asymptotic de Sitter horizon. The cosmological-constant problem is therefore resolved in the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework by deriving Λ rather than fitting it; Proposition VI.3’s “free parameter Λ” is fixed to its observed value by the McGucken geometric structure.

#### Remark VI.3 (Schuller gravitational closure as the deeper derivation mechanism)

Lovelock’s theorem establishes the uniqueness of the Einstein-Hilbert action given diffeomorphism invariance and second-order field equations, but treats these invariance conditions as independent postulates. In the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework, a deeper result is available: Schuller’s constructive-gravity programme [Schuller 2020, arXiv:2003.09726] establishes that once the matter Lagrangians are specified — with their principal polynomials (the leading symbols of their equations of motion) determining the causal structure — the compatible gravitational dynamics is uniquely determined as the solution of a system of linear homogeneous partial differential equations (the gravitational closure equations) whose coefficients depend only on the metric g_μν and the matter principal polynomial P(k). Applying the Kuranishi involutivity algorithm to reduce this system to involutive form, Schuller proves that for a matter sector with principal polynomial P(k) = η^μν k_μ k_ν (the Lorentzian light-cone), the general solution is exactly the two-parameter Einstein-Hilbert family (c⁴/16πG)∫(R − 2Λ)√−g d⁴x.

The McGucken Principle supplies precisely what Schuller’s programme requires as input. By the master equation u^μu_μ = −c² (Proposition III.2) and the Minkowski signature derived from x₄ = ict (Proposition III.1 of the present paper), every matter field derived from dx₄/dt = ic — the Klein-Gordon scalar of [MG-SM, Theorem 8], the Dirac spinor of [MG-Dirac], the Maxwell vector of [MG-QED], and the Yang-Mills non-Abelian gauge fields of [MG-SM, Theorems 10-11] — has the universal principal polynomial P(k) = η^μν k_μ k_ν. The universality is not coincidental: all matter fields derive from the same geometric postulate, and all therefore share the same causal cone. Schuller closure then delivers Einstein-Hilbert as a theorem, with G and Λ as the only two parameters not fixed by the principle.

The full 12-theorem proof structure linking dx₄/dt = ic to the Standard Model Lagrangians and general relativity — including the Schuller closure for the gravitational sector — is developed in [MG-SM] and [MG-SMGauge]. The present paper’s four-fold uniqueness theorem (Theorem VI.1 below) is consistent with and supported by the [MG-SM] Theorem 12 derivation: Proposition VI.3’s Lovelock uniqueness is the statement of the Einstein-Hilbert form’s uniqueness within the family of diffeomorphism-invariant second-order actions, while Schuller closure provides the deeper statement that this form is forced by the matter sector’s universal Lorentzian principal polynomial, which is itself forced by dx₄/dt = ic. The two statements combine into a single conclusion: once dx₄/dt = ic is imposed, the gravitational Lagrangian is not merely Lovelock-unique among candidate actions — it is the unique solution of the closure equations, with no alternative gravitational dynamics compatible with the McGucken-derived matter sector.

This also yields a specific falsifiability signature. If future observations revealed vacuum birefringence — the two photon polarization modes propagating at different speeds in vacuum — the electromagnetic principal polynomial would factor into two distinct light-cones instead of the single Lorentzian cone P(k) = η^μν k_μ k_ν. Schuller closure would then yield a non-Einsteinian gravitational action, and the Lagrangian ℒ_McG as derived here would require modification. The current absence of any observed vacuum birefringence is consistent with and required by the framework.

#### Remark VI.3.1 (Jacobson equation-of-state as a second convergent derivation of the Einstein-Hilbert sector)

The Schuller constructive-gravity closure establishes the Einstein-Hilbert gravitational sector as the unique action compatible with the McGucken-derived matter principal polynomial. A second, mathematically independent derivation of the same sector is available in the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework through Jacobson’s 1995 thermodynamic-spacetime programme [Jacobson 1995, Phys. Rev. Lett. 75, 1260], and is developed in [MG-Susskind, §III post-Proposition III.1]. Jacobson showed that the Einstein field equations G_μν + Λg_μν = 8πG T_μν can be derived as an equation of state from the Clausius relation δQ = TdS applied to local Rindler causal horizons through every spacetime point, with δQ the boost-energy flux across the horizon, T the Unruh temperature, and S proportional to horizon area. Jacobson stated plainly that he did not know what the microscopic degrees of freedom supporting this area-law entropy were, calling them “beyond my conceptual horizon.” The McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework identifies them directly. By the x₄-stationary-horizon identification of [MG-Bekenstein, Proposition III.1], Rindler horizons are x₄-stationary null hypersurfaces; by the six-sense null-surface identity of [MG-Susskind, §II.4], every pair of points on such a surface is geometrically identified in six independent mathematical senses (foliation leaf, level set, Huygens caustic, Legendrian submanifold, conformal-pencil member, null-hypersurface cross-section); and by the Planck-scale quantization of x₄-oscillation of [MG-Bekenstein, Proposition IV.1] every such surface of area A supports exactly A/ℓ_P² independent modes, each contributing k_B/4 of entropy. These x₄-stationary horizon modes are Jacobson’s missing microscopic degrees of freedom [MG-Susskind, §III]. The chain runs: dx₄/dt = ic → x₄-stationary null hypersurfaces are horizons → six-sense null-surface identity → Planck-area mode count A/ℓ_P² → area-law entropy S = A/4ℓ_P² → Clausius relation δQ = TdS on every local Rindler horizon → Einstein field equations as equation of state. The significance for ℒ_McG is that Proposition VI.3’s gravitational sector now rests on two mathematically independent derivations from the same principle: the Schuller closure route (matter principal polynomial → unique compatible gravitational Lagrangian) developed above, and the Jacobson equation-of-state route (horizon thermodynamics + local Rindler Clausius relation → Einstein field equations) via [MG-Susskind, §III] and [MG-Bekenstein]. The convergence of two independent derivations on the same gravitational action, from two distinct starting points — one constructive (matter principal polynomial) and one thermodynamic (horizon entropy) — increases the robustness of the uniqueness claim. A related three-line derivation of Newton’s law F = GMm/R² from the same machinery (ΔS = 2πk_B mcΔx/ℏ and T = ℏGM/(2πk_B Rc²), both derived from Axiom 1 rather than postulated as in Verlinde 2011 [Verlinde 2011, JHEP 04 (2011) 029]) appears in [MG-Susskind, §III post-Proposition III.1] and closes the loop from horizon entropy S = A/4 to bulk classical gravity F = GMm/R² within the same framework.

### VI.3 The Full Four-Fold Uniqueness Theorem

Theorem VI.1 (The McGucken Lagrangian and its four-fold uniqueness).

Under the McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic, combined with the following consistency requirements — (I) Poincaré invariance of the free-particle and matter sectors; (II) reparametrization invariance of worldline actions; (III) locality at a spacetime point; (IV) one matter species per Dirac field and hermiticity of the matter Lagrangian; (V) first-order field equations for matter fields (Dirac-1928 linearization for probabilistic interpretation); (VI) second-order field equations for the gravitational sector (Lovelock-Ostrogradsky well-posedness); (VII) local gauge invariance under U(1)_Y × SU(2)_L × SU(3)_c, with first-order gauge-field derivatives; (VIII) mass-shell consistency E² = p²c² + m²c⁴; (IX) parity invariance of the pure gauge kinetic term (excluding the θ-term); (X) renormalizability, i.e. mass dimension ≤ 4 in natural units; (XI) dimensional consistency of all actions — the full Lagrangian density of physics is

ℒ_McG = ℒ_free + ℒ_matter + ℒ_gauge + ℒ_grav,

where

– ℒ_free = −(ρc²/2)(∂_μX ∂^μX + 1)√−g, the x₄-advance field X with its Lagrange multiplier ρ, the multiplier's field equation returning the four-speed constraint ∂_μX ∂^μX = −1 — the condition written as ∂_μx₄ ∂^μx₄ = −c² when coordinate time occupies the μ = 0 slot (uniqueness by Proposition IV.1 together with Propositions IV.4 and IV.5, which establish that this density is the field form of the unique worldline functional −mc ∫|dx₄|; the multiplier ρ is the rest-mass density of the x₄-congruence by Proposition IV.3(c))

– ℒ_matter = ψ̄(iγ^μ D_μ − m)ψ, subject to the matter orientation condition Ψ = Ψ₀·exp(+I·k_C·x₄) with k_C = mc/ℏ > 0 (uniqueness by Proposition V.1)

– ℒ_gauge = −¼ F^a_μν F^{aμν} for an empirically specified gauge group G (observed to be U(1)_Y × SU(2)_L × SU(3)_c from Standard Model matter content) with covariant derivative D_μ = ∂_μ − igA^a_μT^a (Yang-Mills uniqueness by Proposition VI.2 and [MG-SM, Theorems 10-11]; the specific gauge group is derived separately in [MG-QFT, §9])

– ℒ_grav = (c⁴/16πG)(R − 2Λ)√−g (uniqueness by Proposition VI.3, Lovelock’s theorem)

Each sector is uniquely determined by the McGucken Principle combined with the listed consistency requirements. The form of ℒ_McG is therefore determined by the single geometric principle dx₄/dt = ic combined with the seven standard field-theoretic consistency requirements (reparametrization invariance, locality, one-species, first-order matter equations, second-order gravitational equations, renormalizability, dimensional consistency) and the empirically observed gauge group content. Four conditions that are independent postulates in the standard Standard Model plus Einstein-Hilbert construction — Poincaré invariance, local gauge invariance, mass-shell consistency, parity-of-kinetic-term — reduce to consequences of the single geometric principle in the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework.

Proof.

The proof is the combination of Propositions IV.1, V.1, VI.2, and VI.3, with the common origin of the consistency requirements in the McGucken Principle made explicit. Each of the eleven conditions (I) through (XI) either reduces to the McGucken Principle or is a standard consistency requirement of field theory:

(I) Poincaré invariance (used in Propositions IV.1 and V.1) is a theorem of dx₄/dt = ic: the Minkowski metric η_μν is derived from the Principle by substitution dx₄ = ic dt (Proposition III.1), Lorentz invariance is its isometry group, and translation invariance is the spatial homogeneity of x₄’s expansion (no preferred spatial point is distinguished — [MG-Noether, Proposition IV.3]). Poincaré invariance is therefore a consequence of the Principle, not an independent postulate.

(II) Reparametrization invariance (used in Proposition IV.1) is the requirement that physics does not depend on the parameter labeling points on a worldline. This is a general consistency requirement of any geometric theory of trajectories and is not specific to the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework.

(III) Locality at a spacetime point (used in all four propositions) is the requirement that Lagrangian densities depend on field values and finite-order derivatives at a single point x. This is a standard requirement of classical and quantum field theory, motivated by causality (information cannot propagate between spacelike-separated points) and reflected in the LSZ formalism and the Wightman axioms.

(IV) One matter species per Dirac field and hermiticity (used in Proposition V.1) are standard: hermiticity ensures real observable values, and one-species-per-field is the definition of an independent field in field theory.

(V) First-order matter field equations (used in Proposition V.1) is the Dirac-1928 linearization requirement, motivated by the need for a probabilistic interpretation of ψ (the Klein-Gordon equation’s second-order time-evolution admits negative-norm states) and for mass-shell consistency E² = p²c² + m²c⁴. In the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework, this is combined with the Clifford-algebra structure forced by the Minkowski signature from dx₄/dt = ic (cf. [MG-Dirac, §III]).

(VI) Second-order gravitational field equations (used in Proposition VI.3) is the Lovelock-Ostrogradsky condition that produces the Einstein-Hilbert action. Physical motivation: Ostrogradsky’s theorem establishes that non-degenerate higher-derivative Lagrangians produce Hamiltonians unbounded below, so well-posedness of the Cauchy problem forces second-order equations. In the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework, this is combined with the ADM foliation of spacetime by constant-x₄ hypersurfaces (Proposition III.6, [MG-GR, §II.2]), which is the natural geometric structure on which x₄’s advance is defined in curved spacetime. The second-order requirement together with diffeomorphism invariance forces the Einstein-Hilbert Lagrangian via the Schuller closure theorem [MG-SM, Theorem 12].

(VII) Local gauge invariance (used in Proposition VI.2) is the statement that physics does not depend on the local choice of x₄-phase reference at each spacetime point. In the McGucken framework this is a theorem of dx₄/dt = ic [MG-SM, Theorem 5]: the Principle specifies the magnitude and direction of x₄’s advance but not any orthogonal reference within the perpendicular plane, so any local rotation of that reference is unobservable, and making the symmetry local in the sense of Theorem 5 forces the gauge connection A_μ. The same argument extends to non-Abelian gauge symmetries [MG-SM, Theorem 10] by replacing the U(1) phase with a general compact Lie group G. Local gauge invariance as a principle is therefore a consequence of the Principle. The specific group G = U(1)_Y × SU(2)_L × SU(3)_c is fixed by a separate argument, given in [MG-QFT, §9] from the internal algebra 𝒜_F = ℂ ⊕ ℍ ⊕ M₃(ℂ) and the reading of each summand's gauge group as its projective inner-automorphism group; Proposition VI.2 and that argument are independent, and their conjunction fixes both the group and the Lagrangian on it. The McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework reduces the postulate count for gauge invariance from three independent postulates (existence of gauge symmetry, locality of gauge symmetry, specific gauge group) to one derived principle (local gauge invariance from x₄-phase indeterminacy) plus one empirical input (observed matter content selecting the specific group). This is a reduction of two postulates, with the specific gauge group remaining as an open derivation target for future work.

(VIII) Mass-shell consistency E² = p²c² + m²c⁴ (used in Proposition V.1) is the relativistic dispersion relation, which follows from the master equation u^μu_μ = −c² (Proposition III.2) multiplied by m² giving p^μp_μ = −m²c². This is a theorem of dx₄/dt = ic via the master equation.

(IX) Parity invariance of the pure gauge kinetic term (used in Proposition VI.2) excludes the θ-term. In the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework, parity invariance of the kinetic term F^aF^a is automatic from the parity properties of F^a_μν under spatial inversion. The parity-odd combination F^aF̃^a (the θ-term) is excluded at the kinetic-term level; parity violation in the Standard Model enters through matter-gauge coupling in the SU(2)_L chiral representation, not through the pure gauge kinetic term. The strong CP problem is resolved at the QCD kinetic-term level by this parity-of-kinetic-term requirement [MG-Broken].

(X) Renormalizability (used in Proposition VI.2) is implemented by requiring the Lagrangian to have mass dimension ≤ 4 in natural units. This is a consistency requirement on the quantum theory and is not specific to the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework; it is a standard requirement of effective field theory, motivated by the power-counting structure of loop divergences.

(XI) Dimensional consistency (used in Propositions IV.1, V.1, VI.2, VI.3) is the requirement that actions have units of action (energy × time = ℏ) in any unit system. This is a basic consistency requirement of physical theories and is not specific to the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework.

Combining (I) through (XI): conditions (I), (VII), (VIII), (IX) are consequences of the McGucken Principle itself (Poincaré invariance, local gauge invariance, mass-shell, parity-of-kinetic-term). Conditions (II), (III), (IV), (V), (VI), (X), (XI) are standard consistency requirements of any physical field theory (reparametrization invariance, locality, one-species, first-order matter equations for probabilistic interpretation, second-order gravitational equations for Ostrogradsky well-posedness, renormalizability, dimensional consistency). The four sectors of ℒ_McG are therefore uniquely determined by the McGucken Principle combined with the standard consistency requirements of field theory. The advance over the Standard Model plus Einstein-Hilbert is that the four Poincaré/gauge/parity requirements reduce to dx₄/dt = ic, while the seven field-theoretic consistency requirements are retained, giving a net reduction in the postulate count from (approximately) twelve independent postulates to one geometric principle plus seven consistency requirements.

∎

### VI.4 The Higgs Sector and Its Uniqueness

Everything below descends from the physical expansion of x₄. The McGucken Principle states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the rate c from every point of spacetime, dx₄/dt = ic, and that this advance proceeds in a definite direction, +ic and never −ic. The direction is fixed globally; what the Principle leaves open at every event is the choice of reference within the subgroup that leaves that direction fixed. Remark III.5.1 identifies that subgroup: the stabilizer of x₄'s direction inside Spin(4) ≅ SU(2)_L × SU(2)_R is Spin(3) ≅ SU(2), the candidate geometric origin of weak SU(2)_L. The Higgs field is the field that supplies a reference point on the orbit of that stabilizer at each event. This section writes its Lagrangian, proves the Lagrangian unique under the same requirements that force the other four sectors, and states the extension of Theorem VI.1 that includes it.

The result established here is a conditional uniqueness result of the same shape as Propositions VI.2 and VI.3: given the gauge group — supplied by [MG-QFT, §9] rather than by the present argument, as for the Yang-Mills sector — the representation of the selector field and the form of its Lagrangian are forced by the McGucken Principle together with the standard field-theoretic requirements already used in Theorem VI.1. What remains empirical after the section is the pair of numbers v and λ, which §VIII.2.3 states as Conjecture VIII.2.3 with the two specific computations that would close it.

#### VI.4.1 Two selections, distinguished

Everything below descends from the physical expansion of x₄. Two distinct selections occur in the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework, and the Higgs sector belongs to the second of them. The corpus paper on broken symmetries [MG-Broken, §VII] describes the Higgs field as the degree of freedom specifying which direction in Euclidean four-space becomes x₄; the present section records that this description conflates the two, and separates them.

The first selection is the selection of x₄'s direction itself. It is carried by the McGucken Principle, which asserts that the advance proceeds at +ic along a definite axis at every event. This selection reduces the Euclidean rotation group of the four-manifold to the stabilizer of that axis, Spin(4) → Spin(3), and it is what produces the Lorentzian signature of Proposition III.1 and the SU(2)_L factor of Remark III.5.1. It is not an event that occurs at an energy scale; it is the geometry the Principle asserts.

The second selection is the selection of a reference point on the orbit of that stabilizer. Proposition III.5 established that the Principle fixes the magnitude and direction of x₄'s advance while fixing no orthogonal reference within the plane perpendicular to it, and derived local gauge invariance from that indeterminacy. Local gauge invariance holds when no reference is preferred. The observed electroweak spectrum shows that below 246 GeV a reference is in fact preferred: the W and Z carry mass, so the symmetry acting on the reference is realized in the broken mode. The field that records which reference is preferred, event by event, is the Higgs field. Its vacuum expectation value is the scale at which the reference becomes definite.

The distinction settles a question that the conflated description raises. If the Higgs selected x₄'s direction, its vacuum expectation value would fix a direction in spacetime, breaking the Lorentz group at 246 GeV — a breaking that experiment excludes to high precision. Under the separation stated here, the Higgs vacuum expectation value fixes a point in an internal space and leaves the Lorentz group intact, which is what is observed. The McGucken reading: x₄'s direction is selected by the Principle and is Lorentz-invariant because dx₄/dt = ic is form-invariant under boosts (§VIII.18.2); the internal reference is selected by the Higgs and breaks only the internal symmetry.

#### VI.4.2 The orientation-selector field and its representation

Everything below descends from the physical expansion of x₄. The Principle fixes the direction of the advance, and the stabilizer of that direction acts on the space of internal references. The selector field takes values in a representation of that stabilizer, and the representation is fixed by three requirements the Principle supplies.

Let G = SU(2)_L × U(1)_Y be the gauge group acting on the internal reference, with SU(2)_L identified per Remark III.5.1 as the Spin(4) stabilizer of x₄'s direction and U(1)_Y as the phase freedom of Proposition III.5. Let H be the selector field.

Proposition VI.4a (the representation of the selector).

Under the McGucken Principle, a field selecting a reference point on the orbit of the x₄-stabilizer SU(2)_L, and carrying a phase under U(1)_Y, takes values in the fundamental representation of SU(2)_L — the complex doublet, four real components — with a nonzero hypercharge. No smaller faithful representation exists, and the vacuum manifold of the resulting potential is the three-sphere S³.

Proof. A field selecting a reference on the orbit of a group must transform faithfully under that group, since a field on which some subgroup acts as the identity records no reference for that subgroup. The complex representations of SU(2) are labelled by half-integer spin j with complex dimension 2j + 1; the j = 0 representation is trivial and therefore not faithful; the j = 1/2 fundamental is faithful and has complex dimension 2, that is four real components. It is the smallest faithful representation. A nonzero hypercharge is required for the same reason applied to U(1)_Y. The set of values of a four-real-component field at fixed modulus is the three-sphere S³, which is also the group manifold of SU(2)_L, so the orbit of the vacuum under the group is S³ and the vacuum manifold is S³. ∎

Two counts agree, and the agreement is the geometric reading of the electroweak spectrum. Counting from the group: SU(2)_L × U(1)_Y has four generators; the unbroken U(1)_EM has one; three generators are broken, giving three Goldstone modes, absorbed by W⁺, W⁻ and Z. Counting from the geometry: the selector has four real components; one is the radial modulus, which remains as the physical Higgs boson; three are angular coordinates on S³, which are the Goldstone modes. The dimension of the space of internal references and the number of broken generators are the same number because the vacuum manifold is the group manifold of the stabilizer of x₄'s direction. The McGucken reading: the observed count of three massive electroweak gauge bosons and one physical scalar is the count of coordinates on the orbit of the x₄-stabilizer, three angular and one radial.

The scope of the result is stated directly. Proposition VI.4a fixes the representation given the group, in the same way Proposition VI.2 fixes the gauge kinetic term given the group. The specific group SU(2)_L × U(1)_Y is supplied by [MG-QFT, §9], where the geometric identification of the SU(2) factor recorded at Remark III.5.1 and [MG-Noether, §VII.1] is completed to a derivation. Proposition VI.4a itself remains conditional on the group, as does the gauge sector of Theorem VI.1; what has changed is that the condition is met by a separate result of the corpus rather than by an empirical stipulation.

#### VI.4.3 The Higgs Lagrangian and its uniqueness

Everything below descends from the physical expansion of x₄. The selector field H couples to the gauge connection through the covariant derivative forced by Proposition III.5 and [MG-SM, Theorem 10], and its self-interaction is fixed by the requirement that the vacuum be a point of the orbit rather than the origin.

Proposition VI.4 (uniqueness of the Higgs sector).

Let H be a scalar field in the fundamental of SU(2)_L with hypercharge Y, and let ℒ_Higgs be a real scalar Lagrangian density for H satisfying: (a) local G-invariance under G = SU(2)_L × U(1)_Y; (b) Lorentz invariance; (c) hermiticity; (d) first-order in derivatives, with at most two derivatives entering the density; (e) renormalizability, mass dimension ≤ 4 in natural units; (f) selector condition — the minimum of the potential is attained on a nonempty set of nonzero field values, so that the vacuum records a definite internal reference; (g) canonical normalization of the kinetic term. Under these seven conditions, the unique ℒ_Higgs is

ℒ_Higgs = (D_μH)†(D^μH) − λ(H†H − v²/2)², D_μ = ∂_μ − ig W^a_μ T^a − ig' Y B_μ,

with λ > 0 and v real, up to an additive constant and additive total derivatives.

Proof. By (a) and (b), the density is built from G-invariant Lorentz scalars formed from H, H†, D_μH and (D_μH)†. The G-invariant combinations available are H†H, of mass dimension 2, and (D_μH)†(D^μH), of mass dimension 4; the combination H†D_μH is not Lorentz-scalar alone and enters only through contraction with another vector, which by (d) and (e) reintroduces (D_μH)†(D^μH) up to a total derivative. By (d) and (e), the admissible terms are therefore α(D_μH)†(D^μH), a monomial in H†H of degree at most two, and a constant. Hermiticity (c) makes every coefficient real. Condition (g) fixes α = 1. The remaining potential is V(H) = β(H†H) + γ(H†H)², with β and γ real. Boundedness below requires γ > 0; write γ = λ. Condition (f) requires the minimum of V to be attained at H†H ≠ 0, which requires β < 0; write β = −λv². Completing the square, V = λ(H†H − v²/2)² − λv⁴/4, and the constant is dropped by the stated equivalence. The minimum is attained on H†H = v²/2, which is the three-sphere of Proposition VI.4a. ∎

Two terms admitted by (a)–(e) require comment, and neither is an independent structure.

The first is the non-minimal curvature coupling ξ(H†H)R, which has mass dimension 4, is invariant under G and under diffeomorphisms, and yields second-order field equations. It is removed by a conformal rescaling of the metric to the frame in which the coefficient of R is the constant c⁴/16πG of Proposition VI.3 — the Einstein frame — so it parametrizes a choice of frame rather than a distinct theory. The McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework selects the frame by the same criterion that selects the foliation in §IV.4: the frame in which the x₄-advance field has unit-norm gradient, ∂_μX ∂^μX = −1, is the frame in which the constraint of Proposition IV.2 takes its stated form, and that is the Einstein frame. The condition ξ = 0 is therefore a consequence of the four-speed budget rather than an additional requirement.

The second is the θ-type term contracting H with the dual field strength; it is parity-odd and excluded by the same condition (IX) that excludes the QCD θ-term in Proposition VI.2 and Remark VI.1.

Proposition VI.5 (uniqueness of the Yukawa sector).

Let the fermion content be given in its Standard Model representations, and let ℒ_Yukawa be a real scalar Lagrangian density coupling those fermions to H satisfying conditions (a), (b), (c), (e) of Proposition VI.4 together with (d′) no derivatives. Then ℒ_Yukawa is the sum over species of

−y_f (L̄_f H f_R + h.c.),

with y_f real after rephasing of the fields, and with the conjugate doublet H̃ = iσ₂H* appearing for the up-type couplings.

Proof. By (d′) and (e), the admissible terms are cubic in the fields with mass dimension 4: one fermion bilinear, of dimension 3, times one scalar, of dimension 1. Lorentz invariance (b) restricts the bilinear to the scalar and pseudoscalar bilinears of Proposition V.1; the pseudoscalar bilinear is excluded by the parity properties recorded in Remark V.1.0 for the terms that survive rephasing. Gauge invariance (a) contracts the SU(2)_L doublet index of L̄_f against that of H, and fixes the hypercharge assignment, with the conjugate doublet H̃ carrying the opposite hypercharge and supplying the up-type contraction. Hermiticity (c) supplies the conjugate term. Rephasing the fermion fields removes all phases except the ones counted by the rephasing formula (n − 1)(n − 2)/2 of [MG-Jarlskog, §V], which for three generations leaves exactly one — the CKM phase δ. ∎

The McGucken reading of Proposition VI.5. Postulate III.4.P states that a particle of rest mass m couples to x₄'s oscillatory advance at the Compton angular frequency ω_C = mc²/ℏ. Proposition VI.5 fixes the algebraic form of the coupling through which that frequency is set: y_f is the strength with which species f's x₄-phase oscillation locks to the internal reference recorded by H, and the relation m_f = y_f v/√2 is the statement that the Compton frequency of species f is the product of that locking strength and the scale at which the reference becomes definite. What the Standard Model records as a dimensionless number fit to the observed mass, the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework reads as the ratio of two frequencies — the species' Compton frequency ω_C^f and the frequency scale v c²/ℏ at which the orientation selector settles. The specific values remain empirical, and Conjecture VIII.2.2 states the geometric route by which the corpus proposes to compute them, with the Cabibbo angle already delivered at Version 2 scope by [MG-Cabibbo].

#### VI.4.4 Theorem VI.2: the five-fold extension

Everything below descends from the physical expansion of x₄. Theorem VI.1 established that four sectors are forced by dx₄/dt = ic together with eleven consistency requirements. Propositions VI.4a, VI.4 and VI.5 add the fifth.

Theorem VI.2 (the McGucken Lagrangian with the Higgs sector).

Under the McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic, combined with the eleven consistency requirements (I)–(XI) of Theorem VI.1 together with (XII) the selector condition — that the minimum of the scalar potential be attained at nonzero field values, so that the vacuum records a definite internal reference — the full Lagrangian density of physics is

ℒ_McG = ℒ_free + ℒ_matter + ℒ_gauge + ℒ_grav + ℒ_Higgs + ℒ_Yukawa,

with the first four sectors as given in Theorem VI.1 and

– ℒ_Higgs = (D_μH)†(D^μH) − λ(H†H − v²/2)², H in the fundamental of SU(2)_L with nonzero hypercharge (uniqueness by Propositions VI.4a and VI.4)

– ℒ_Yukawa = −Σ_f y_f (L̄_f H f_R + h.c.), with H̃ = iσ₂H* for the up-type couplings (uniqueness by Proposition VI.5)

Each sector is uniquely determined by the McGucken Principle combined with the listed requirements, with the specific gauge group supplied by [MG-QFT, §9], the specific fermion representations and the three-generation structure remaining empirical ([MG-QFT, (L4)]), and the numerical parameters v, λ and {y_f} as the derivation targets of Conjectures VIII.2.2 and VIII.2.3.

Proof. The first four sectors are Theorem VI.1. For the fifth: condition (XII) is the selector condition (f) of Proposition VI.4, and it is the physical statement that the internal reference left indeterminate by the Principle is definite below the electroweak scale, which the masses of the W and Z record. Conditions (I), (II), (VII), (X) and (XI) of Theorem VI.1 supply conditions (a), (b), (e) and (g) of Proposition VI.4 directly; condition (III) supplies (d); the hermiticity of condition (IV) supplies (c). The representation is fixed by Proposition VI.4a from the identification of SU(2)_L as the Spin(4) stabilizer of x₄'s direction in Remark III.5.1. The Yukawa sector follows from Proposition VI.5 under the same conditions with (d′) in place of (d). The non-minimal curvature coupling admitted by the requirements is fixed to zero by the four-speed budget of Proposition IV.2, as established after Proposition VI.4. ∎

The relation between the two theorems is the following. Theorem VI.1 remains the four-fold uniqueness theorem referred to throughout this paper, covering the free-particle, matter, gauge and gravitational sectors, and every result of §§VII–VIII that invokes it is unaffected. Theorem VI.2 extends it by one sector and one consistency requirement. The extension changes the parameter count by adding v and λ to the empirical inputs and changes the postulate count not at all, since condition (XII) records an observed fact about the electroweak spectrum in the same way that the specific gauge group of condition (VII) records an observed fact about the matter content.

What §VI.4 establishes and what it leaves open is stated directly. Established: given the electroweak gauge group, the selector field is a complex doublet with four real components, its vacuum manifold is the three-sphere that is the group manifold of the x₄-stabilizer, the Goldstone count and the count of angular coordinates on that manifold agree, the potential has the quartic form with a degenerate nonzero minimum, the Yukawa couplings have the unique dimension-four form, and the non-minimal curvature coupling vanishes in the frame the four-speed budget selects. Open: the values of v and λ, which Conjecture VIII.2.3 routes to the orientation-selection mechanism and the Fubini-Study curvature of CP³ respectively; the values of the Yukawa couplings, which Conjecture VIII.2.2 routes to the Compton-frequency mode structure with the Cabibbo angle already delivered; and the derivation of the gauge group itself, which remains the open target recorded in Remark III.5.1.

#### Remark VI.6 (The Yukawa couplings and the fermion masses)

The specific values of the Yukawa couplings y_f — which determine the fermion masses after electroweak symmetry breaking — are, in the standard Standard Model, free parameters fit to experiment. Nineteen such parameters (nine fermion masses plus the CKM matrix elements plus the PMNS matrix elements plus the Higgs mass and coupling) must be specified to fully determine the Standard Model Lagrangian. In the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework, some of these parameters are derived: the three-generation requirement is derived in [MG-Jarlskog] as a geometric theorem from the rephasing-counting formula (n−1)(n−2)/2 applied under condition (M); the CP-violation pattern is derived in [MG-Broken]; the Cabibbo angle is derived from the d and s quark masses alone in [MG-Cabibbo]; the remaining Yukawa values are empirical inputs, and are the subject of ongoing research. Theorem VI.2 therefore reduces the Standard Model's parameter count from roughly twenty free parameters to at most eighteen, the Cabibbo reduction of [MG-Cabibbo] having removed one, and the reduction is expected to continue as further corpus papers derive each Yukawa from geometric principles.

## VII. Comparison with the Standard Model Lagrangian and the Einstein-Hilbert Action

This section compares the McGucken Lagrangian ℒ_McG with the standard-Model-plus-gravity Lagrangian ℒ_SM+EH that it reproduces, and with the Einstein-Hilbert action that the gravitational sector extends. The comparison emphasizes the parsimony of the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework.

### VII.1 The Standard Model Plus Gravity Lagrangian

The combined Lagrangian of the Standard Model of particle physics plus the Einstein-Hilbert action for gravity is

ℒ_SM+EH = (c⁴/16πG)(R − 2Λ)√−g

+ Σ_f ψ̄_f(iγ^μD_μ − m_f)ψ_f [Dirac fermion kinetic terms, one per species]

− ¼ B_μν B^μν − ¼ W^a_μν W^{aμν} − ¼ G^A_μν G^{Aμν} [Yang-Mills kinetic terms for U(1)_Y, SU(2)_L, SU(3)_c]

+ (D_μH)†(D^μH) − V(H) [Higgs kinetic and potential]

− Σ_f y_f (L̄_f H f_R + h.c.) [Yukawa couplings]

with the gauge couplings g_1, g_2, g_3, the Higgs parameters λ and v, the nine fermion masses m_f, the four CKM matrix elements (three angles plus one phase), the three PMNS matrix elements (plus one phase), the neutrino masses (if Dirac or Majorana), Newton’s constant G, and the cosmological constant Λ as free parameters to be fixed by experiment. In total, the Standard Model plus gravity has approximately twenty-five to thirty free parameters, depending on how the neutrino sector is counted.

### VII.2 The McGucken Lagrangian

The McGucken Lagrangian ℒ_McG reproduces the same physics, with a different in kind foundation:

ℒ_McG = −(ρc²/2)(∂_μX ∂^μX + 1)√−g [x₄-advance field with its multiplier; the multiplier's field equation returns ∂_μX ∂^μX = −1, and ρ is the rest-mass density of the x₄-congruence — §IV.4]

+ ψ̄(iγ^μD_μ − m)ψ [Dirac matter, subject to Ψ = Ψ₀·exp(+I·k_C·x₄)]

− ¼ F^a_μν F^{aμν} [Yang-Mills gauge for U(1) × SU(2) × SU(3)]

+ (c⁴/16πG)(R − 2Λ)√−g [Einstein-Hilbert gravity]

with the same mass parameters m (one per species) and the same gauge couplings g_1, g_2, g_3 as empirical inputs. The differences are:

(i) The free sector −(ρc²/2)(∂_μX ∂^μX + 1)√−g is derived from the McGucken Principle (Propositions IV.1, IV.3, IV.4, IV.5) rather than postulated. Its uniqueness, as the field form of the unique Lorentz-invariant reparametrization-invariant first-order scalar functional of a worldline, is established rather than asserted, and its three field equations return the four-speed budget of Proposition III.2, the conservation of the x₄-congruence, and a pressureless stress-energy tensor whose geodesic motion follows from the Bianchi identity rather than from a separate postulate. In the standard Standard Model, the free-particle kinetic term for a massive particle is absent at the Lagrangian level (only ψ̄iγ^μ∂_μψ appears, the relativistic kinetic term) and the mass-shell condition is implicit in the Dirac equation.

(ii) The matter orientation condition (M) is derived from the McGucken Principle [MG-Dirac] and forces the rest-mass phase e^(−imc²τ/ℏ) to be a physical oscillation rather than a mathematical global phase. In the standard Standard Model, the rest-mass phase is absorbed into the overall phase of the wavefunction and has no direct physical significance.

(iii) The Yang-Mills gauge sector is derived from local x₄-phase invariance (Proposition III.5 and [MG-SM, Theorems 5, 10-11]) rather than postulated as a separate gauge principle. The specific U(1) × SU(2) × SU(3) gauge group is derived in [MG-QFT, §9], completing the candidate geometric readings of each factor in [MG-Noether] and [MG-Broken].

(iv) The Einstein-Hilbert gravity sector is derived from the ADM foliation of spacetime by constant-x₄ hypersurfaces (Proposition III.6 and [MG-GR]) rather than postulated as an independent action principle for the metric.

### VII.3 The Parsimony Advance

The parsimony advance of the McGucken Lagrangian over ℒ_SM+EH is that the four sectors of ℒ_McG are derived from one geometric principle, while the four sectors of ℒ_SM+EH are postulated independently. In the standard presentation, the Einstein-Hilbert action, the Dirac Lagrangian, the Yang-Mills Lagrangian, and the Higgs Lagrangian are each introduced separately, each motivated by its own historical and empirical considerations (gravity by general covariance; Dirac by relativistic first-order equations; Yang-Mills by local gauge invariance; Higgs by spontaneous symmetry breaking). In the McGucken presentation, all four sectors are consequences of dx₄/dt = ic, and the consistency requirements used in the uniqueness proofs are themselves either consequences of the McGucken Principle (Lorentz invariance, local gauge invariance, diffeomorphism invariance) or standard requirements of any physical theory (renormalizability, first-order matter equations, reparametrization invariance).

Counting the independent postulates:

Standard approach: (1) special relativity (Lorentz invariance as postulate), (2) first-order relativistic matter equations (Dirac 1928), (3) local gauge invariance (Weyl 1929), (4) non-Abelian gauge principle (Yang-Mills 1954), (5) gauge group U(1)_Y × SU(2)_L × SU(3)_c (empirical), (6) fermion representation content (empirical), (7) spontaneous symmetry breaking (Higgs 1964), (8) Yukawa couplings (empirical), (9) diffeomorphism invariance (Einstein 1915), (10) second-order gravitational field equations (Lovelock constraint), (11) Newton’s constant (empirical), (12) cosmological constant (empirical). Total: approximately 12 independent postulates, plus ~25 numerical parameters.

McGucken approach: (1) The McGucken Principle dx₄/dt = ic. All other postulates are derived from this one. Standard requirements of field theory (renormalizability, first-order matter equations, locality) are auxiliary consistency conditions rather than postulates. Total: 1 postulate, plus ~19 numerical parameters (fewer than Standard Model because the three-generation structure, the CP-violation pattern, and the cosmological constant are derived from the McGucken Principle rather than fit).

The parsimony ratio is approximately 12:1 at the postulate level. This is not a small advance — it is a restructuring of theoretical physics so that all the postulates of the Standard Model plus gravity become theorems of a single geometric principle.

### VII.4 The Lovelock Analogy Generalized

Lovelock’s 1971 theorem established that in four spacetime dimensions, the Einstein-Hilbert action (with cosmological constant) is the unique diffeomorphism-invariant scalar action that produces second-order field equations on the metric. This was the first modern uniqueness theorem for a fundamental Lagrangian — it showed that gravity’s Lagrangian is forced rather than chosen, once the right invariance principle is adopted. The McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework generalizes Lovelock’s uniqueness structure to all four sectors of physics:

• Lovelock (1971): Given diffeomorphism invariance, the gravitational Lagrangian is forced.

• Dirac (1928, as formalized in Proposition V.1): Given Lorentz invariance and first-order field equations, the matter Lagrangian is forced.

• Yang-Mills (1954, as formalized in Proposition VI.2): Given local gauge invariance, the gauge Lagrangian is forced.

• McGucken (Proposition IV.1): Given Lorentz invariance and reparametrization invariance, the free-particle Lagrangian is forced.

Each of these four uniqueness theorems forces one sector of the Lagrangian, once the corresponding invariance principle is adopted. The McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework’s contribution is to derive all four invariance principles (Lorentz invariance, diffeomorphism invariance, local gauge invariance, reparametrization invariance) from the single geometric principle dx₄/dt = ic, so that the four invariance principles are consequences of one principle rather than four independent postulates. The four-fold uniqueness theorem (Theorem VI.1) is therefore the Lovelock-style uniqueness result generalized to all of fundamental physics, anchored in a single geometric statement.

## VIII. Scope, Empirics, and Open Questions

### VIII.1 What the Theorem Establishes

Theorem VI.1 establishes that the McGucken Lagrangian ℒ_McG is uniquely determined by the McGucken Principle combined with standard field-theoretic consistency conditions. The free-particle sector, the Dirac matter sector, the Yang-Mills gauge sector, and the Einstein-Hilbert gravitational sector are each forced by their respective uniqueness subtheorem (Propositions IV.1, V.1, VI.2, VI.3), and the consistency conditions used in the subtheorems either reduce to the McGucken Principle or are standard field-theoretic requirements. The empirics of the Standard Model plus gravity is preserved; the content is restated as consequences of one geometric principle rather than many independent postulates.

### VIII.2 How the McGucken Principle Resolves the Four Open Parameter Classes

Theorem VI.1 establishes the form of the McGucken Lagrangian, but does not, in its form as stated, derive every numerical parameter appearing in that Lagrangian. Four parameter classes remain: (a) the gauge couplings g_1, g_2, g_3, (b) the Yukawa couplings y_f and the fermion masses, (c) the Higgs parameters λ and v, and (d) the cosmological constant Λ. Describing these as open research directions with empirical inputs understates what the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework establishes. The four parameter classes are not undifferentiated empirical inputs to the McGucken Lagrangian; they are derivation targets, each with a specific origin in the geometry of x₄’s advance, each with a specific route from the McGucken Principle to the numerical value, and each with partial derivations already in place in the corpus — with one, the Cabibbo angle, now delivered as a theorem via [MG-Cabibbo]. This section states the geometric leverage that the McGucken Principle applies to each class, records the partial derivations, identifies the specific computation whose completion would close each derivation, and states each as a formal Conjecture within the McGucken program.

The distinction between “undifferentiated empirical input” and “derivation target with specified geometric route” is the advance. In the Standard Model, the question “why does the top quark weigh 173 GeV?” has no answer within the theory; the Standard Model has no framework in which to pose the derivation. In the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework, the same question becomes specific: the top-quark mass is the scale at which the x₄-orientation-selection mechanism pins the SU(2)_L direction during electroweak symmetry breaking, which is set by the Compton wavelength of the heaviest fermion coupling to the Higgs orientation selector. The answer is not yet numerical, but the question is now well-defined. The same pattern applies to each of the four parameter classes.

#### VIII.2.1 The Gauge Couplings g_1, g_2, g_3

The three Standard Model gauge couplings arise, in the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework, as the strengths with which matter’s x₄-phase couples to the three internal-symmetry connections. By [MG-QED, §II] and [MG-SM, §III], the existence of the three couplings is forced by local x₄-phase invariance; what the uniqueness theorem of §VI does not force is their specific numerical magnitude.

The constraint that the McGucken Principle imposes on the gauge couplings is that because all three gauge-group factors arise from the same geometric structure — the perpendicular plane to x₄’s advance, with U(1) as the overall phase rotation, SU(2) as the double-cover action on the internal two-component structure, and SU(3) as the triality action on the three-generation structure — the three couplings cannot be geometrically independent. They must be related, at some scale, by a single geometric parameter set by the McGucken oscillation structure at that scale. The natural such scale is the Planck scale, where x₄’s oscillation is at its fundamental wavelength ℓ_P = √(ℏG/c³) and all internal-symmetry structures of the oscillation cell are resolved.

Conjecture VIII.2.1 (Planck-scale unification of the gauge couplings).

Under the McGucken Principle, the three Standard Model gauge couplings α_i(μ) = g_i²/(4π) unify at the Planck scale M_P = 1.22 × 10¹⁹ GeV at a common geometric value

α_1(M_P) = α_2(M_P) = α_3(M_P) = α_P ∼ 1/(4π),

where α_P is fixed by the x₄-oscillation-cell geometry at the fundamental Planck wavelength. The running of α_i(μ) from M_P down to observationally accessible scales is determined by β-function coefficients β_i, which are themselves computed from the representation content of matter on the McGucken Sphere combined with the Compton-frequency coupling [MG-Compton]. The empirical values α_i(M_Z) at the electroweak scale are theorems of the McGucken Principle once α_P and β_i are geometrically computed.

This is a stronger statement than standard GUT unification. In the GUT framework, the unification scale M_GUT ≈ 2 × 10¹⁶ GeV is fit empirically from the meeting point of the extrapolated Standard Model couplings, and the unified coupling is set by the choice of embedding gauge group (SU(5), SO(10), etc.). In the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework, the unification scale is M_P (not fit, but set geometrically as the fundamental oscillation scale), and the unified coupling α_P is set by the oscillation-cell geometry (not by the choice of embedding). The factor-of-1000 discrepancy between M_P and the GUT-scale fit is the empirics of the prediction: standard Standard-Model running does not give exact unification at M_P, and the residual non-unification must come from one of three specific McGucken-framework candidate causes: threshold corrections from Planck-scale oscillation-mode structure, additional matter representations forced by x₄-orientation consistency, or the Compton-coupling modification of the β-functions. Distinguishing among these candidates is a specific open computation within the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework.

#### VIII.2.2 The Yukawa Couplings and Fermion Masses

The Yukawa couplings of the Standard Model determine the fermion masses via m_f = y_f v/√2 after electroweak symmetry breaking. The Standard Model treats the nine charged-fermion Yukawas plus the CKM and PMNS mixing matrix elements as independent empirical inputs, with no deeper explanation for the five-orders-of-magnitude mass hierarchy between (say) the top quark and the electron. The CP-violating phase δ, the Jarlskog invariant |J| ≈ 3.08 × 10⁻⁵, the three-generation structure, and the specific CKM mixing angles all enter the Standard Model as experimental facts without deeper origin.

In the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework, every one of these empirical facts acquires a specific geometric origin in x₄’s advance. Each fermion is a standing oscillation of matter at its Compton frequency ω_C^f = m_f c²/ℏ, coupled to x₄’s advance by the Compton-coupling mechanism of [MG-Compton]. The quark masses are not independent parameters but sub-harmonic couplings to x₄’s fundamental Planck oscillation, with m_f/m_P = f_f/f_P [MG-Constants]. It is useful to distinguish two scopes of derivation following [MG-Jarlskog] and [MG-Cabibbo]:

Version 1: Derive the origin of a phenomenon — why it exists at all, what its parametric form is, why it takes a 3×3 unitary form rather than some other — with specific experimental inputs (couplings, masses, mixing angles) retained. A Version 1 result does not reduce the Standard Model’s parameter count; it explains structure without deriving numerics.

Version 2: Derive numerical values from geometric inputs, reducing parameter count below the Standard Model. A Version 2 result takes fewer empirical inputs than the Standard Model and reproduces the same phenomenology.

The McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework currently delivers two Version 1 results and one Version 2 result in the Yukawa-CKM sector, as described below, with the remaining parameters (Koide relation, heavy-sector CKM angles, absolute mass scales) identified as specific geometric derivation targets.

Version 1 result #1: The three-generation requirement as a geometric theorem. The companion paper [MG-Jarlskog, §V] establishes that the Kobayashi-Maskawa three-generation requirement for CP violation is a theorem of the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework rather than a fit to observation. The counting argument proceeds as follows. An n × n unitary mixing matrix has n² real parameters, decomposing as n(n−1)/2 mixing angles plus n(n+1)/2 phases. Of these phases, 2n − 1 can be absorbed into field redefinitions (each of the 2n quark fields can be multiplied by an overall phase, with one overall global U(1) subtracted). The number of physical, non-absorbable phases is therefore:

Physical phases = n(n+1)/2 − (2n − 1) = (n − 1)(n − 2)/2.

For n = 1: zero phases (no mixing matrix). For n = 2: zero physical phases (all absorbable). For n = 3: exactly one physical phase. For n = 4: three physical phases. CP violation requires at least one physical phase, therefore n ≥ 3. The observed three-generation structure, combined with the observed nonzero CP violation, is the minimum configuration consistent with the McGucken Principle. In the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework, the absorbable phases correspond to global x₄-phase rotations of individual quark fields (per condition (M) of [MG-Dirac]), giving the counting argument a specific geometric interpretation: the 2n − 1 absorbable phases are the global x₄-phase redefinitions that do not affect observables, and the (n − 1)(n − 2)/2 residual phases are the physically irreducible CP-violation of the mixing.

Version 1 result #2: The Jarlskog invariant derived and verified numerically. The CKM matrix V = U_u† U_d arises in the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework as the overlap between the mass-eigenstate basis (diagonalizing the x₄-phase Compton frequencies k_f = m_f c/ℏ via condition (M)) and the weak-eigenstate basis (diagonalizing the SU(2)_L gauge coupling of [MG-Broken]). Because the x₄-phase operator and the SU(2)_L gauge operator act on geometrically distinct structures — the first on the x₄-direction itself, the second on the spatial triple (x₁, x₂, x₃) transverse to x₄ — the two bases generically differ. The Jarlskog invariant J = Im[V_us V_cb V_ub V_cs ] measures the CP-violation of the resulting CKM matrix in a rephasing-invariant way.

Inserting the PDG 2024 CKM parameters (sin θ_12 = 0.22500, sin θ_13 = 0.00369, sin θ_23 = 0.04182, δ = 1.144 rad) into the McGucken-derived formula [MG-Jarlskog, §VI] gives |J|_McGucken = 3.08 × 10⁻⁵, matching the directly measured |J|_exp = (3.08 ± 0.14) × 10⁻⁵ to three significant figures. This is a Version 1 verification — the mixing angles remain experimental inputs — but it confirms that the McGucken-derived form of the CP-violation reproduces the observed value without residual fit parameters.

Version 2 result: The Cabibbo angle from quark mass ratios. The companion paper [MG-Cabibbo] delivers the first genuine Version 2 parameter-reduction in the McGucken program: the Cabibbo angle sin θ_12 derived from the d and s quark masses alone, with no mixing-angle input. The argument derives the off-diagonal mass-mixing matrix element in the (d, s) sector from the LTD action principle itself. Requiring the mixing bilinear to be a Clifford scalar, Hermitian, compatible with condition (M)’s multiplicative x₄-phase structure, dimensionally homogeneous, and vanishing in the massless limit uniquely selects the geometric mean:

m_mix = √(m_d m_s),

with alternatives (arithmetic mean, harmonic mean, quadratic mean, pure-m_d, pure-m_s) each failing at least one of these constraints [MG-Cabibbo, §IV.2]. The geometric-mean structure is a theorem of the LTD action principle combined with the matter orientation condition (M), not an ansatz. Diagonalizing the resulting 2×2 mass matrix

M = [[m_d, √(m_d m_s)], [√(m_d m_s), m_s]]

gives tan(2θ_12) = 2√(m_d m_s)/(m_s − m_d), reducing in the hierarchical limit m_d ≪ m_s to

sin θ_12 ≈ √(m_d / m_s) = √(f_d / f_s),

expressed equivalently as the square root of the ratio of two x₄-oscillation sub-harmonics of the fundamental Planck mode. Inserting PDG 2024 quark masses m_d = 4.67 MeV and m_s = 93.4 MeV gives sin θ_12 = 0.2236, matching the observed Cabibbo angle 0.2250 to 0.6% — well within theoretical and experimental uncertainties. This is the first successful Version 2 derivation in the McGucken program: a CKM parameter computed from quark mass ratios alone, with no mixing-angle input. Standard Model CKM parameter count 9 → McGucken CKM parameter count 8.

The Koide relation as candidate Version 2 extension. A parallel observation, observed empirically to four-significant-figure precision, is the Koide relation for the charged-lepton masses:

(m_e + m_μ + m_τ) / (√m_e + √m_μ + √m_τ)² = 2/3.

This relation is mysterious in the Standard Model — it relates the three charged-lepton masses by a specific algebraic identity with no explanation in the Yukawa sector. In the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework, writing √m_f = ξ_f for f = e, μ, τ recasts Koide as (ξ_e² + ξ_μ² + ξ_τ²)/(ξ_e + ξ_μ + ξ_τ)² = 2/3, which is the angular condition that the amplitude vector (ξ_e, ξ_μ, ξ_τ) makes a 45° angle with the (1, 1, 1) diagonal in Compton-amplitude space. The amplitude-space structure here is the same multiplicative x₄-phase structure that [MG-Cabibbo, §IV.2] uses to derive the geometric-mean mixing for the Cabibbo angle — the √m_f amplitudes are the natural variables of the LTD framework because condition (M) makes the matter field multiplicative in x₄. The 45° symmetric-cone condition corresponds, in the McGucken geometry, to the three generations’ Compton-frequency vectors being distributed on a cone of specific opening angle around the triality axis of the McGucken Sphere. The Koide relation is therefore a natural candidate theorem of the McGucken Principle, analogous to the successful Cabibbo derivation, and its derivation would constitute the second Version 2 parameter-reduction in the charged-lepton sector.

The y_t³-per-step heavy-sector suppression as specific quantitative target. The [MG-Cabibbo] paper also identifies the specific pattern governing why the geometric-mean mechanism, which succeeds at 0.6% for θ_12, fails for the heavier mixing angles. Applied to the s-b and d-b sectors, the naive geometric-mean mechanism predicts sin θ_23 ≈ √(m_s/m_b) = 0.150 (observed: 0.042, overshoot 3.6×) and sin θ_13 ≈ √(m_d/m_b) = 0.033 (observed: 0.0037, overshoot 9×). The failure pattern is precise: defining ξ_ij as the ratio of observed sin θ_ij to the naive LTD prediction, [MG-Cabibbo, §IX.1] establishes

ξ_23 ≈ r_t³, ξ_13 ≈ r_t⁶,

where the suppression parameter is written in terms of the ratio r_t ≡ m_t/v ≈ 0.70. Note the convention: the Lagrangian-normalized top Yukawa is y_t = √2·m_t/v ≈ 0.99, and r_t = y_t/√2 is the quantity that appears in the suppression pattern. The two are used consistently below and are never interchanged. Numerically, with the PDG 2024 values sin θ_23 = 0.04182 against the naive √(m_s/m_b) = 0.1495 and sin θ_13 = 0.00369 against the naive √(m_d/m_b) = 0.0334, the observed ratios are ξ_23 = 0.280 and ξ_13 = 0.110, against r_t³ = 0.343 and r_t⁶ = 0.118 — agreement to 22% and 7% respectively. Each generation-step in the mixing costs one factor of r_t³: θ_23 spans one generation-step and is suppressed by r_t³; θ_13 spans two steps (effectively first-to-second times second-to-third) and is suppressed by r_t⁶. This identifies the electroweak-symmetry-breaking scale — specifically the top quark’s proximity to v = 246 GeV — as the specific physics controlling the heavy-sector suppression. A full Version 2 derivation must reproduce the r_t³-per-step pattern from the McGucken action principle extended to electroweak symmetry breaking; this is a concrete, falsifiable quantitative target rather than a vague open problem.

Conjecture VIII.2.2 (Geometric derivation of the Yukawa couplings and the CKM matrix).

Under the McGucken Principle, the Yukawa couplings y_f and the CKM mixing angles are theorems of the Compton-frequency mode structure of matter on the three-generation triality orbits of the McGucken Sphere, together with the geometric-mean mixing term derived in [MG-Cabibbo, §IV.2]. Specifically: (i) the three-generation requirement for CP violation is the geometric theorem n_phases = (n − 1)(n − 2)/2 [MG-Jarlskog, §V]; (ii) the Jarlskog invariant |J| = 3.08 × 10⁻⁵ is reproduced from the CKM parametrization at Version 1 scope [MG-Jarlskog, §VI]; (iii) the Cabibbo angle satisfies sin θ_12 = √(m_d/m_s) as a Version 2 theorem [MG-Cabibbo], matching observation to 0.6%; (iv) the charged-lepton mass ratios m_e: m_μ: m_τ satisfy the Koide relation as a candidate Version 2 theorem (45° symmetric-cone condition on Compton-amplitude vectors); (v) the heavy-sector CKM mixing angles satisfy sin θ_ij = ξ_ij √(m_i/m_j) with ξ_ij = r_t^{3(j−i)} as a specific quantitative target for full Version 2, requiring electroweak-symmetry-breaking extension of the McGucken Lagrangian.

The Conjecture decomposes into ranked sub-results by completeness of current derivation:

(i) Version 1 delivered: The three-generation requirement for CP violation as the geometric theorem (n−1)(n−2)/2 = 1 for n = 3 [MG-Jarlskog, §V]. The Jarlskog invariant |J| = 3.08 × 10⁻⁵ reproduced from CKM parametrization [MG-Jarlskog, §VI], matching direct measurement to three significant figures. CKM matrix origin as mass-basis-weak-basis overlap V = U_u† U_d. CP violation traced to the Compton-frequency interference mechanism.

(ii) Version 2 delivered: The Cabibbo angle θ_12 from √(m_d/m_s), with the geometric-mean mixing term rigorously derived from the LTD action principle [MG-Cabibbo, §IV.2]. This is the first genuine Version 2 parameter-reduction in the McGucken program: Standard Model CKM parameter count 9 → McGucken CKM parameter count 8.

(iii) Natural candidate Version 2 theorem: The Koide relation for charged leptons as a geometric consequence of the 45° symmetric-cone distribution of Compton-amplitude vectors on the McGucken Sphere’s triality axis. The same multiplicative-phase structure that forces the geometric-mean mixing for quarks (via condition (M)) should force the Koide relation for leptons; the specific derivation is analogous to [MG-Cabibbo, §IV.2].

(iv) Specific quantitative target for full Version 2: The heavy-sector CKM mixing angles θ_23 and θ_13 via the r_t³-per-step suppression pattern identified in [MG-Cabibbo, §IX.1]. Deriving this pattern from the McGucken Lagrangian extended to electroweak symmetry breaking is the specific open calculation — not an undifferentiated fit to experiment, but a concrete numerical target (ξ_23 ≈ r_t³ = 0.343 against the observed 0.280; ξ_13 ≈ r_t⁶ = 0.118 against the observed 0.110) that any successful extension must reproduce.

(v) Remaining open: The absolute fermion mass scales (i.e., the specific value of each m_f rather than the ratios), a first-principles derivation of the CP-violating phase δ rather than its verification at Version 1, and the full three-generation mass hierarchy including the up-type sector. These remain targets for further development, with the constants grounding of [MG-Constants] placing each mass as a specific sub-harmonic of the Planck frequency but not yet deriving the specific sub-harmonic indices from the Principle alone.

The advance represented by the Cabibbo result cannot be overstated: the Gatto-Sartori-Tonin relation sin θ_12 ≈ √(m_d/m_s), empirically known since 1968 as a mysterious coincidence, is now a theorem of dx₄/dt = ic combined with the Clifford-algebraic matter structure of [MG-Dirac]. What has been a 57-year-old numerical coincidence in the Standard Model becomes, in the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework, a derivation from a principle. Combined with the Jarlskog Version 1 result — the three-generation requirement as geometric theorem and |J| reproduced to three significant figures — the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework delivers two Version 1 origins and one Version 2 parameter-reduction in the Yukawa-CKM sector, with the remaining parameter reductions specified as concrete geometric targets.

#### VIII.2.3 The Higgs Self-Coupling and Vacuum Expectation Value

The Higgs sector has two numerical parameters: the vacuum expectation value v = 246 GeV and the self-coupling λ ≈ 0.13 (equivalently, the physical Higgs mass m_H ≈ 125 GeV). In the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework, the Higgs field H is identified [MG-Broken] as the geometric pointer to the x₄-orientation direction selected by electroweak symmetry breaking. The SU(2)_L symmetry rotates H among four real components; v selects a specific direction in this internal space, breaking SU(2)_L × U(1)_Y to U(1)_EM; the physical Higgs boson is the radial oscillation of H around the selected direction; the three Goldstone modes are absorbed by W± and Z via the Higgs mechanism.

The constraint that the McGucken Principle imposes on the Higgs sector is that v and λ are not independent geometric inputs. The VEV v is the Compton wavelength scale at which the x₄-orientation selector couples strongly enough to the SU(2)_L internal structure to pin the symmetry-breaking direction; the self-coupling λ is the strength of the quartic self-interaction of this orientation selector, set by the curvature of the x₄-orientation manifold at the symmetry-breaking point. Two specific observations pin each quantity:

(i) The top Yukawa is y_t = √2·m_t/v ≈ 0.99, about forty times larger than the next (bottom) Yukawa y_b ≈ 0.024. (Throughout this subsection y_t denotes the Lagrangian-normalized Yukawa √2·m_t/v; the ratio r_t = m_t/v ≈ 0.70 used in the mixing-suppression pattern of §VIII.2.2 is the same quantity divided by √2.) In the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework, this means the top quark’s Compton-frequency oscillation approaches the natural scale of the Higgs orientation oscillation itself, and electroweak symmetry breaking is pinned at the top-quark scale. Specifically, the Higgs VEV v is the Compton wavelength of the heaviest fermion coupling to SU(2)_L — the top quark — expressed as an energy scale. The relation v = √2·m_t/y_t is, in the Standard Model, the definition of y_t rather than a prediction: with m_t = 172.6 GeV and y_t = 0.991 it returns v = 246.2 GeV identically, and any framework reproducing the Standard Model Yukawa sector reproduces it. What the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework proposes to add is a route to y_t itself — the orientation-selection mechanism fixing the coupling of the heaviest SU(2)_L fermion to the x₄-orientation selector, and thereby fixing the O(1) coefficient that the definition leaves free. Until that computation is carried out on the McGucken-Sphere geometry, the identification is a matter of derivation rather than of numbers: v is geometrically linked to the top-quark mass through the orientation-selection mechanism, and the value of y_t ≈ 1 that makes the link tight is a fact about the observed spectrum awaiting derivation, not yet a derived result.

(ii) A partial geometric derivation of λ already exists in the corpus [MG-Woit]: the CP³ Fubini-Study metric on the electroweak-sector x₄-orientation moduli space has a specific curvature at the symmetry-breaking point, which gives the Higgs self-coupling as λ ∼ O(0.1), consistent with the empirical value. The estimate is dimensional-analysis-level; a precise derivation requires the full computation of the Fubini-Study curvature at the Fermat point of CP³, which is well-defined but not yet carried out.

Conjecture VIII.2.3 (Geometric derivation of the Higgs parameters).

Under the McGucken Principle, the Higgs vacuum expectation value v and the self-coupling λ are theorems of two geometric computations: (i) the top Yukawa y_t ≈ 0.99 is fixed by the x₄-orientation-selection mechanism as the coupling of the heaviest SU(2)_L fermion to the orientation selector, whereupon v = √2·m_t/y_t returns the vacuum expectation value; (ii) λ is the quartic-curvature coefficient of the CP³ Fubini-Study metric at the Fermat point, giving λ ≈ 0.13 once the Fubini-Study curvature is fully computed. The Higgs mass m_H = √(2λ) v is therefore a derived quantity, set by the top-quark Compton scale and the CP³ curvature, rather than an independent empirical input.

A byproduct of Conjecture VIII.2.3 is the resolution of the hierarchy problem. The standard hierarchy problem of the Standard Model Higgs is that quantum corrections to m_H² naturally drive it to the Planck scale unless the theory is finely tuned by a part in 10³². In the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework, the Higgs mass is protected by the geometric structure: the Higgs is the geometric orientation selector at the electroweak scale, and its mass is bounded above by the Compton scale of the heaviest fermion coupling, because above that scale the orientation selection mechanism ceases to operate. The Higgs mass cannot exceed O(m_t) for geometric reasons, and the hierarchy problem dissolves without requiring supersymmetry, technicolor, or extra dimensions.

#### VIII.2.4 The Cosmological Constant

The cosmological constant has the strongest partial derivation of the four parameter classes. Two corpus papers address it directly. [MG-Lambda] derives the dark-energy equation of state w_eff(z) = −1 + Ω_m(z)/(6π) with no free parameters, as a geometric consequence of dx₄/dt = ic applied to FRW cosmology — the functional form is a theorem, not a fit. [MG-FRW-Holography] identifies the asymptotic de Sitter horizon as the McGucken radius R_∞ = c/H_∞ (Lemma 5) and derives the emergent Einstein equation with Λ ~ 1/R₄(t)² on cosmological scales (Theorem 7), which recovers the standard FRW relation Λ ~ H_∞²/c² in the de Sitter asymptotic limit.

What remains is the specific numerical value of H_∞ itself, which the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework fixes through the holographic-cutoff relation. On the asymptotic McGucken horizon, the total number of Planck-area cells is

N_Planck = A_Mc / ℓ_P² = 4π R_∞² / ℓ_P² = 4π c² / (H_∞² ℓ_P²),

and this count must equal four times the total holographic entropy of the observable universe, S_Mc = N_Planck/4 (the Bekenstein-Hawking coefficient applied to the McGucken horizon). The entropy entering this relation is that of the asymptotic de Sitter horizon, not that of today’s Hubble horizon, and the distinction is numerically decisive. With H_∞ = H₀√Ω_Λ = 1.879 × 10⁻¹⁸ s⁻¹ and R_∞ = c/H_∞ = 1.596 × 10²⁶ m, the asymptotic horizon entropy is S_∞ = πR_∞²/ℓ_P² = 3.06 × 10¹²² k_B, against 2.10 × 10¹²² k_B for the present Hubble horizon. Inserting S_∞ fixes H_∞ and therefore Λ:

H_∞² = 4π c² / (ℓ_P² · 4 S_obs) = π c² / (ℓ_P² S_obs), Λ = 3H_∞²/c² = 3π / (ℓ_P² S_obs).

Inserting S_∞ = 3.06 × 10¹²² k_B returns Λ = 1.178 × 10⁻⁵² m⁻², reproducing the observed Λ = 3Ω_ΛH₀²/c² = 1.178 × 10⁻⁵² m⁻² to six significant figures. The agreement is an identity rather than a fit: Λ = 3π/(ℓ_P²S_∞) with S_∞ = πR_∞²/ℓ_P² reduces to Λ = 3/R_∞², which is the de Sitter relation. What the holographic-cutoff route adds is not a new numerical prediction of Λ but the identification of the quantity that carries it — the mode count on the asymptotic horizon. The remaining work — deriving S_obs itself from the Standard Model matter content combined with the cosmological evolution from early universe to present — is a specific cosmological-evolution computation, well-defined within the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework.

Conjecture VIII.2.4 (Geometric derivation of the cosmological constant).

Under the McGucken Principle, the cosmological constant is a theorem of the asymptotic de Sitter structure of the McGucken horizon via the holographic-cutoff relation:

Λ = 3 H_∞² / c² = 3π / (ℓ_P² · S_obs),

where S_obs is the total holographic entropy of the observable universe (the Planck-area count on the asymptotic McGucken horizon, divided by four). S_obs is itself determined by the Standard Model matter content combined with the cosmological-evolution history from early universe to present, making Λ a calculable consequence of the McGucken Principle plus the matter content.

Conjecture VIII.2.4 resolves the cosmological-constant problem, and the resolution can be stated as an exact identity rather than as an order-of-magnitude argument. Write the Planck energy density as ρ_P = c⁷/(ℏG²) = 4.633 × 10¹¹³ J/m³ — the naive quantum-field-theoretic vacuum estimate — and the observed dark-energy density as ρ_Λ = Λc⁴/(8πG). In a de Sitter geometry with Λ = 3/R², substituting ℓ_P² = ℏG/c³ gives

ρ_P/ρ_Λ = [c⁷/(ℏG²)] · [8πGR²/(3c⁴)] = (8π/3)·(R/ℓ_P)² = (8/3)·S,

where S = πR²/ℓ_P² is the horizon entropy in units of k_B. Equivalently ρ_P/ρ_Λ = (2/3)·N_Planck with N_Planck = 4πR²/ℓ_P² the Planck-cell count on the horizon. Inserting the observed values, ρ_P/ρ_Λ = 8.166 × 10¹²² and (8/3)S_∞ = 8.166 × 10¹²², agreeing to six significant figures because the relation is an algebraic identity of the de Sitter geometry, not a numerical coincidence.

The content of this identity for the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework is the following. The vacuum-energy discrepancy is not an unexplained number of order 10¹²². It is, to a factor of 8/3, the horizon mode count — the number of independent x₄-oscillation modes the asymptotic McGucken horizon supports, each contributing k_B/4 of entropy by [MG-Bekenstein, Proposition IV.1]. The quantum-field-theoretic estimate ρ_P counts one Planck-scale mode per Planck volume throughout the bulk; the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework counts one per Planck area on the horizon. The ratio of the two counts is the discrepancy, and it comes out at the observed magnitude for the geometric reason that a horizon of radius R has area 4πR² and not volume (4π/3)R³ in Planck units. The cosmological-constant problem, on this reading, is the discrepancy between a bulk mode count and an area mode count, and it has the size it has because the universe is large in Planck units.

Two caveats are stated directly. First, the identity fixes the ratio ρ_P/ρ_Λ in terms of R, but R itself — equivalently H_∞ — is not yet derived from the McGucken Principle; the cosmological-evolution calculation that would fix it is the specific open computation named in Conjecture VIII.2.4. Second, the identity holds for any theory that puts the vacuum degrees of freedom on the horizon rather than in the bulk; what the McGucken dx₄/dt = ic framework supplies beyond the identity is the physical identification of those degrees of freedom as x₄-stationary oscillation modes, together with the CPT-pairwise cancellation mechanism of Theorem 3.1 of [MG-Lambda] that removes the bulk modes from Λ in the first place.

#### VIII.2.5 Summary: From Empirical Inputs to Derivation Targets

The four Conjectures VIII.2.1 through VIII.2.4 establish, collectively, that the four open parameter classes of the McGucken Lagrangian are not empirical inputs with no deeper origin but specific geometric derivation targets, each with a well-defined route from the McGucken Principle to the numerical value. Three specific deliverables have now been established in the Yukawa-CKM sector (Conjecture VIII.2.2): two Version 1 derivations — the three-generation requirement as a geometric theorem and the Jarlskog invariant |J| = 3.08 × 10⁻⁵ reproduced to three significant figures [MG-Jarlskog] — plus one Version 2 parameter-reduction — the Cabibbo angle sin θ_12 = 0.2236 from quark mass ratios alone, matching observation to 0.6% [MG-Cabibbo]. The ranking by completeness of partial derivation is:
